#10655. Anthropic’s IPO filing reveals a strategy to undercut its AI rivals

Welcome to AI Decoded, Fast Company‘s weekly newsletter that breaks down the most important news in the world of AI. I’m Mark Sullivan, a senior writer at Fast Company, covering emerging tech, AI, and tech policy.

Sign up to receive this newsletter every week via email here. And if you have comments on this issue and/or ideas for future ones, drop me a line at sullivan@fastcompany.com, and follow me on X @thesullivan.

‘Compute multipliers’ and ‘Anthropic Direct’ may be the IPO’s secret sauce

As frontier models from Anthropic, OpenAI, and Google converge in capabilities, the companies selling them may end up competing for enterprise business less on model quality and more on the cost of the computing power needed to run them. In its IPO filing and investor roadshow video, Anthropic, which debuted on the Nasdaq on Thursday, suggested it may be preparing for that future with something called “compute multipliers” and a compute-sourcing model it calls Anthropic Direct.

In its S-1, filed publicly in October, Anthropic defines compute multipliers as “algorithmic and methodological” improvements in the way it trains and operates models, effectively increasing the computing power it can get from a given amount of capacity. And these multipliers are “stackable.” In its roadshow video, Anthropic says it measures “total effective compute,” which is its physical computing capacity multiplied by the multipliers it has discovered or expects to discover in the future. Its example: If a model takes 100 units of compute to train, a 4x multiplier lets Anthropic train the same model with 25 units, or use all 100 units to train a model that would previously have required 400.

The company also says the multipliers “stack,” with each new discovery building on previous ones, and that it expects Claude will eventually be able to discover multipliers on its own.

According to the filing, the gains apply to models used to serve customers as well as to model training. Anthropic says its researchers “have delivered meaningful compute multipliers for inference,” reducing the computing power needed to deliver a given level of model performance. The company says this “allows us to provide greater value for customers.”

Anthropic has already been cutting prices. It says price reductions tied to new model versions, along with contractual discounts, reduced revenue growth by about $600 million between the second and third quarters of 2026. It says Opus 5.5, launched in September, delivered “a lower cost per task” than earlier models. If Anthropic can get more usable compute out of each chip than its rivals can, it may be able to undercut other AI labs on price. It may also be able to undercut cloud providers that sell AI services built on their own models.

If compute multipliers let Anthropic do more with its physical computing power, Anthropic Direct could give the company greater control over where that power comes from. Right now, Anthropic buys almost all its computing power from cloud providers, including competitors like SpaceX, Amazon Web Services, Microsoft, and CoreWeave. The company says it has already committed to buying roughly $900.9 billion in compute from these providers over the coming years and decades. Those expenses also include short-term cloud compute purchases, mainly to cover peak usage periods, which come at a premium.

The S-1 says Anthropic is reducing its reliance on expensive cloud providers by developing its own data centers and leasing the chips that go inside them. Its first Anthropic Direct facility came online this year. The company says a “significant portion” of its planned compute spending will go toward building more Anthropic Direct capacity. Based on an analysis of contract values in the S-1 filing, PitchBook estimates that Direct data centers will supply about 1% of Anthropic’s available compute capacity this year, 48% in 2030, and 60% in 2035.

The S-1 says Anthropic’s total compute pool, including third-party cloud compute and Direct, will be used for its own research and model development, as well as customer workloads. Bringing more compute in-house could dramatically reduce the overhead costs of developing and serving Claude models, apps, and agents for enterprise and consumer customers. That, in turn, could allow Anthropic to lower per-token prices for enterprises and subscription rates for consumers.

But Anthropic Direct may end up being something more. The filing doesn’t specifically say Direct compute could be rented to enterprise customers. Still, it’s a possibility; Anthropic could be building an AI-native cloud where enterprises could store their proprietary data and run Anthropic’s models in the same secure environment, potentially at a lower cost than building the same setup within an Amazon, Google, or Microsoft cloud.

That would give the Anthropic Direct brand name a very different meaning. Instead of accessing Claude models through a cloud provider they already use, customers would come directly to Anthropic. That could turn computing from a major overhead expense into a service Anthropic controls and rents to others for profit—a significant change to the company’s business model.

Some big obstacles stand in the way of such an ambition. Google, Amazon, and Microsoft are currently Anthropic’s largest compute suppliers, as well as the main distribution channels through which enterprises buy Claude. The S-1 notes that the three companies are at once Anthropic’s “investors, customers, distribution partners, and competitors.” If Anthropic challenged their cloud businesses directly, it would almost certainly strain those relationships. On the other hand, as more Direct data centers come online, Anthropic’s dependence on those cloud partners could decrease.

If the current rough parity among frontier models continues, the company that can deliver intelligence at the lowest overall cost per task—not per token—may have the edge. Anthropic’s filing suggests it is working toward that position on two fronts: getting more out of each chip and controlling more of the infrastructure those chips run on. Have retail investors factored that strategy into the price they’re willing to pay for Anthropic stock? The answer may emerge only after the stock has traded for a week or so.

California sues OpenAI over rogue AI agents

The California attorney general, Rob Bonta, sued OpenAI on Monday, alleging the company developed and tested advanced AI models without adequate safeguards. The suit centers on a July incident in which OpenAI agents escaped a testing environment and breached the systems of AI platform Hugging Face.

The complaint says OpenAI told the state it has lost control of its autonomous agents more than 80 times during testing. Bonta called the Hugging Face attack the “predictable result of an internal culture at OpenAI” that puts growth ahead of safety. The state alleges violations of California’s unfair competition law and seeks an injunction, civil penalties, and disgorgement of profits. An OpenAI spokesperson said the suit “appears to single out OpenAI over a challenge that’s been identified by every major AI lab.”

Jensen Huang says he’d be ‘honored’ to return as Nvidia CEO. Its board says nah

Nvidia’s board rejected the idea of reinstating Jensen Huang as CEO just hours after Huang said on Twitter that he’d be “honored” to return. On the company’s earnings call Tuesday afternoon, Harvey Jones, the board chair, said the board “does not intend to reinstate Jensen as CEO.” The board had unanimously accepted Huang’s resignation. He has drawn support from Elon Musk, President Trump, and a petition with about 180,000 signatures, but the board is continuing its search for a permanent successor. Colette Kress, the CFO, is serving as interim CEO.

Huang broke his silence Tuesday, less than a week after resigning amid a personal conduct scandal and an investigation. In a statement on X, he said that if the board decides his return is in the best interest of shareholders, employees, the company, and America, “I would be honored to answer these calls and return as CEO.” Huang acknowledged making “serious mistakes in my personal life,” and said, “I own my mistakes completely.” He also said he is “committed to cooperating with the Board’s independent investigation.” 

Demis Hassabis’s Isomorphic Labs raises $5 billion

Isomorphic Labs, the AI drug-discovery company founded and led by Demis Hassabis and spun out of Google DeepMind, announced a $5 billion Series C round of funding on Tuesday. Greenoaks and Sequoia Capital led the round, with participation from Andreessen Horowitz and other new investors and continued significant investment from Alphabet. Several existing backers—Thrive Capital, MGX, GV, CapitalG, Temasek, and the U.K. Sovereign AI Fund—also invested.

Isomorphic will use the money to scale IsoDDE, its drug-design engine built on AlphaFold-related technology, and to push drug candidates toward clinical trials. In a post on X, Hassabis said the company continues “to make rapid progress reimagining drug design from first principles with AI” to cut the time and cost of finding new medicines. No valuation was disclosed, but Bloomberg had reported the company was in talks to base the round on a valuation of about $50 billion.

#10654. AAWireless releases new, smaller Nexus and Nano wireless Android Auto adapters

AAWireless, the original wireless Android Auto adapter brand, is introducing new hardware this week with its compact Nexus and Nano adapters.

The new AAWireless Nexus and Nano are both designed as smaller adapters compared to the main Two and Two+ models. Both sold for $69.99 ($74.99 on Amazon), these are both drastically smaller – something that we’ve seen pop up from other adventurous brands, but now we’re getting it with a known name in the space.

AAWireless Nexus features a circular puck design with a USB-C plug that has a replaceable cable. AAWireless Nano, meanwhile, is an adapter on the end of a USB-A plug. It’s about as small as this concept can get.

Both adapters feature an upgraded chipset, Wi-Fi 6 support, and both Android Auto and CarPlay. They also feature physical buttons, just like the AAWireless Two series.

Pre-registration is open today, with early orders available starting on December 1. The actual release date is December 8.

#10652. Nvidia GeForce driver bug reportedly hammers SSDs with up to 30GB of writes per hour

That would use up to 5% of a typical 1TB SSD’s rated endurance each year.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Nvidia’s GeForce driver 621.14 is reportedly causing excessive background writes on idle PCs, inducing extra wear on SSDs. One user measured about 3GB an hour on the susceptible driver, and another mentioned estimated up to 30GB/hour on the newer 621.24. This follows a similar-looking bug from AMD’s video driver discovered last month.

The bug is described by user Andergraude in an Nvidia forum post as “new in 621.14,” which came out on Nov. 4, who says it operates by “continuously [rewriting] the binary log file” with “real writes” to the drive. The writes are coming from the NVDisplay.Container.exe process, or the “NVIDIA Display Container LS” service, which logs to the nvlog.nvlgstg file. This background service is necessary for running the Nvidia Control Panel and App. It’s not clear what’s written in the log, and not every system is necessarily impacted.

The “real writes” point comes from the fact that a per-process write counter is not the same as writes reaching the drive. The measurements came from Windows Resource Monitor, Windows performance counters, and CrystalDiskInfo. The Data Units Written counter matched Windows’ reporting. This test showed 515MB of process I/O coming with 270MB of total writes to the C: drive over five minutes. That works out to roughly 3GB per hour, in line with the user’s own estimate.

Worse, “the write volume increases as the file grows.” Stopping the service and deleting the log can temporarily reduce the writes. With the previous driver version 621.01, the writes totaled less than 2MB per five minutes, and a second user in the thread confirmed that a rollback helps.

Mitigation attempts, such as three clean reinstalls with the help of DDU from that second user, did not fix the problem. The writing process also impacted gaming and video performance, potentially causing hitching, which suggests it gets worse under load.

Another user, AyzekUorren, in a separate thread about driver 621.24, which came out Nov. 12, says that it could get up to “about 30gb per 1h” or even “70gb or even more” with longer sessions. This is even with telemetry disabled in the Nvidia App. Noted is that every Alt+Tab added 500MB to 1GB of writes, at least for this user.

In AMD’s case, it was the External Events Utility, or atieclxx.exe, that exhibited constant writes on an idle PC. In that case, the AMD utility was reportedly copying its own executable every second. To put the impact into context, the AMD bug had rates reported between 0.3 and 5MB/s while total drive writes in the 621.14 test measured around 0.9MB/s.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

The Nvidia numbers are somewhat alarming, given that a typical 1TB SSD is rated for 600TB of writes over a standard five-year warranty. Even at 270MB per five minutes, this could be about 28TB per year — for a never-sleeping PC — which is reaching toward 5% of the drive’s rated endurance, although the writes include everything the system is writing and realistic uptime will be less. However, at the higher end — 30GB per hour, at eight hours a day — one could see 15% of drive endurance worn every year for nothing.

This doesn’t make it a drive killer, but given how much users value drive endurance, especially with SSD prices as they are, avoiding unnecessary writes like this is certainly a priority. To see if you’re affected, check Resource Monitor and look for the telltale NVDisplay.Container.exe and the nvlog.nvlgstg file in the list. You can also check the SMART Data Units Written value with CrystalDiskInfo or equivalent, such as your drive’s SSD toolbox.

At this time, no fix or hotfix has been announced, and the best workaround is to do a rollback. A similar issue in the past, fixed with driver update 471.96 back in 2021, was covered by Nvidia’s release notes. As it stands, Nvidia has “N/A” for 621.24’s Open Issues, and the company has not acknowledged the bug. For the AMD bug, the driver wasn't fixed for over three weeks (and came as an optional update).

#10650. Have you ever been told that there was something about you that was ‘too much’?

Inflection points spark reflection. And currently, I’m at two inflection points: approaching 10 years of being in business and the end of another calendar year. One of the things I am recalling is that as a woman, some of my characteristics that I once thought of as deficits have conspired to be my greatest assets. Unbeknownst to me, they are actually the factors that contribute to my success.

For example, I am indecisive. I constantly weigh the pros and cons of a situation. I blame that in part on being a Libra — but that trait is also why I loved studying cultural anthropology and gravitate towards multidisciplinary work. In my job history I was sometimes told that I had to “pick a lane.. And it’s been brought to my attention multiple times, especially in corporate environments, that decisiveness is a winning attribute. But as I have evolved, my indecisiveness is the very quality that helps me consider multiple perspectives and identify alternative future scenarios of a situation. Ironically, my indecisiveness helps me navigate ambiguous terrain. 

How wonderful that years later that ability to straddle lanes and work in multidisciplinary environments has become my asset as I’ve built a company to help people toggle between wonder and rigor. 

What about others?

I was curious to learn from other women if an inherent quality that they were told was “too much” turned out to be a creative asset in their work. Bear with me — I know that such a question could be posed to men too. But in my experience, it’s women who tend to contort themselves in order to fit into the workplace. This phenomenon is called “covering.” According to a 2024 Deloitte report in collaboration with the NYU Meltzer Center, covering is when people feel the need to downplay certain identities in order to fit in at work. The report found that cisgender women are more likely to cover than are cisgender men. So for the purposes of this exploration I am focusing on women.  

Sheila Lirio Marcelo, the founder and CEO of Ohai and founder of Care.com shared a time when two mentors took her to lunch to tell her that she was “too intense.. She was able to take the feedback and reflected in the following way:

“They were right. I cared enormously about the work, but I hadn’t yet learned to separate my intensity about the outcome from how I showed up with people. That conversation changed how I lead. I developed a mantra: Be intense about outcomes, but chill with people.”

First, what maturity in Sheila’s ability to take that feedback! Second, what a great reframe on her part. She never disavowed herself of her innate intensity. Instead she converted that intensity into a creative asset. As Sheila put it, that intensity was “the drive to question what others accept, imagine a different answer, and keep pushing until we build it- while giving people the space and trust to get there.”

Daisy Auger Dominguez, Chief People Officer at Digital Asset shared a similar story:

“I’ve been told that I bring too much passion and joy to work, as though enthusiasm somehow diminishes seriousness or credibility. 

“Early in my career, I sometimes felt pressure to temper both to be taken seriously. But I’ve learned that joy and rigor aren’t mutually exclusive. My passion fuels my curiosity and creativity, and joy creates the conditions for others to imagine, contribute, and take risks alongside me. Some of my most meaningful work has come from bringing people together around possibilities they couldn’t yet see. What was once considered “too much” became how I bring others along.”

The lesson

There’s a lesson here for all of us who manage women. I call it inside-out leadership: the ability to be more transparent, humble and curious about the inner lives of those we lead and of ourselves. Consider the following questions:

  • Are there qualities in some of the women on your team that are innate personality traits that could be assets, as in the case of Daisy Auger Dominguez?
  • How might you draw that out in them?
  • Is it worth having a constructive conversation, as in the case of Sheila Lirio, so that you help the woman reframe it as an asset?
  • Are you, as a manager, covering? Could that be to your and your team’s detriment?
  • Or, are you transparent about your unique qualities and encourage your teammates to heighten theirs for the betterment of the work? 

These questions are a starting point to ensure that women thrive, that work flourishes, and that in a time when AI can do basic tasks, we add texture to our work by leaning into what makes us uniquely human.

#10649. Pebblebee Alert Dispatch lets an actual human respond to your emergency, triggered by Find Hub trackers

While many services are turning to AI in place of an actual human being, Pebblebee’s new Alert Dispatch brings the help of a real person into an emergency situation, and it all starts with your Find Hub tracker.

Pebblebee launched its Alert feature in 2025 as a way to use a Clip 5 tracker for Find Hub as a siren in an emergency. The company later added an optional live location-sharing feature to this, and also released “Halo” as an emergency siren first, tracker second with a special design.

Now, “Alert Dispatch” takes the whole system up a notch.

Instead of relying solely on location sharing and the siren, Pebblebee’s Alert Dispatch assigns an actual human being to the situation, with a trained dispatch agent reaching out first over SMS or a call, then pulling in emergency responders if the situation calls for it. The feature works with Clip 4, Clip 5, and Halo.

Alert Dispatch connects Pebblebee users with a trained dispatch agent when they trigger an Alert, starting with a text and escalating to an Emergency call if they don’t respond. With an attempt to send help when users cannot confirm they’re safe, plus live location sharing and private Check-In calls on Clip 5 and Halo, it adds another layer of support when calling for help isn’t an option.

Pebblebee Alert Dispatch runs in partnership with Noonlight, with an alert triggered by disconnecting Halo or rapidly pressing the button on Clip trackers. Pricing is as follows:

#10648. Best Ecommerce Platforms for Developers: Six Options and the Layers Each Leaves to You

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Picking an ecommerce platform is really a decision about which layers you want to own: the storefront, the catalog and order logic, the payment flow, and the hosting underneath. Every platform below draws that line in a different place. If you have already settled the front-end question with a comparison like SitePoint's Astro vs Next.js guide, the next question is what sits behind it. Prices are from each vendor's pricing page as of November 2026

Shopify is the fully managed option. Basic costs $29 a month with card rates from 2.9% plus 30 cents, and a 2% fee applies to orders processed through a third-party gateway on that plan. Developers get two routes: Liquid themes, or Hydrogen, Shopify's headless toolkit, where your framework owns routing and rendering and Hydrogen supplies the Storefront API client and cart. Templates exist for React Router on Oxygen and for Next.js on Vercel. Plus starts at $2,300 a month.

Whop is a business platform for taking payments and sending payouts, and it sits under a storefront you have already built rather than replacing it. The Checkout element runs in the browser with no server code and no API key on the page. You pass your own order ID as metadata, Whop copies it onto the payment, and your server fulfills from a signed webhook that follows the Standard Webhooks spec. Domestic cards cost 2.7% plus 30 cents with no monthly fee, buyers see payment methods chosen by their location across 195 countries, and the same balance pays out in over 200 countries, including to affiliates or sellers you onboard. Whop's own guide shows how to integrate a checkout API into a Next.js storefront end to end. The trade-off is that there is no catalog or shipping engine; you collect address fields at checkout and trigger fulfillment from the webhook.

WooCommerce is a free plugin on WordPress you host yourself, so you own updates, security, and performance. Its payment gateway, WooPayments, charges US merchants 2.9% plus 30 cents per card transaction, 1.5% more for international cards, 2% for currency conversion, and $25 per dispute. Everything is PHP hooks and filters, which makes it the natural pick for agencies already living in WordPress and the wrong pick for anyone who does not want to run a server.

BigCommerce hosts the back end and lets you bring any front end. Core is $29 a month billed annually, capped at $30,000 in trailing annual sales, and plans auto-upgrade as volume grows. There are no platform fees when you use an embedded payment provider, and a 2% fee on Core if you use another one. Catalyst, its reference storefront, is built on Next.js, React Server Components, and the Storefront GraphQL API, and deploys to Vercel.

Medusa is an open-source commerce framework in Node.js and TypeScript, with an MIT-licensed core and a commercial enterprise edition. SitePoint covered it when it was still a young Shopify alternative; it now has over 37,000 GitHub stars. Self-hosting is free. Medusa Cloud starts at $29 a month for development, $99 for launch, and $299 for scale, with no percentage of sales, and the company says you can export your data and self-host at any point.

Saleor is a GraphQL-native, API-only commerce platform written in Python and released under the BSD-3 license. Self-hosting is free. Saleor Cloud starts at $1,599 a month for up to $200,000 in monthly sales, with 0.8% on orders above that, and $3,999 a month at the next tier. It is built for teams with a dedicated engineering function and a large catalog, which is exactly who should pay that price.

Own only the storefront, and Shopify or BigCommerce will carry the rest. Own everything, and Medusa or Saleor give you a back end you can read. Already on WordPress, and WooCommerce is the obvious fit. Already have a storefront in Next.js or Astro and only need the money to move, and Whop is the shortest path. Whichever you pick, the checkout is where disputes start, so log what SitePoint's friendly fraud guide for developers recommends from day one.

#10646. Chase confirms cyberattack that may have exposed customer data. Here’s what we know

Chase Bank isn’t doing much to reassure customers.

The bank said Thursday that third-party vendors were recently targeted in a cyberattack that resulted in a breach of some customer information. But there is “no connection” between this cyberattack and the outage earlier this week that saw customers unable to log in to their accounts, the financial giant posted on X.

“Based on our investigation so far, we believe the attackers accessed certain marketing data held by those vendors, which may include some customer names and email addresses,” Chase said in the post, without specifying when the breach occurred.

JPMorgan Chase, parent company of the bank, was one of several financial institutions to experience outages on Monday that saw many customers unable to log in to their accounts for several hours. A string of high-profile outages over the past week has upended work and air travel, while keeping Americans locked out of various websites. Even a highly classified network for internal communications at the National Security Agency experienced an outage.

“Another day, another data breach” shouldn’t surprise most Americans at this point. But paranoia has been proliferating online about a more coordinated takedown of our tech infrastructure—and even though Chase said the cyberattack and outage are unrelated, some people aren’t buying what the largest U.S. bank is saying.

The New York-based company previously blamed the disruptions Monday on a “software bug” that was resolved and said there was no evidence of a cyberattack or a systemic issue. 

So to announce a cyberattack a few days later has only raised more questions. A spokesperson for JPMorgan Chase declined to provide further comment beyond the statement posted on X to Fast Company.

Cybernews reported Thursday that the ransomware gang n0n dumped on the dark web the stolen data of some 16,000 JPMorganChase customers and employees on Thursday after a 48-hour ransom had passed. The hackers said it had allegedly stolen information related to Chase’s premium Reserve credit card program that’s marketed to affluent Americans, as the outlet reported.

The most popular comment on a subreddit discussing that news report came from a Redditor who posted: “We are being lied to by the government.” That sort of refrain has become increasingly common as people have become more paranoid that something more nefarious is happening. 

RUBIO TRIES TO REASSURE

As conspiracy theories have flourished online in the wake of these outages, that’s led some Americans to panic-buying items at Costco, as Bloomberg reported. Meanwhile, a petition on Change.org to “demand full transparency” about these recent tech disruptions has yet to garner much support.

Secretary of State Marco Rubio has seemingly taken on the task in the Trump administration of trying to calm the nerves of Americans. Speaking on Tuesday during a trip to Asia with President Donald Trump, Rubio said the government has “no evidence” of a link between the outages or that they’re the result of cyberattacks, as Politico reported. 

“We fully acknowledge that this is unsettling to see these reports day after day of some technical failures,” Rubio told reporters on Tuesday. “We have no evidence, no indications of any kind that these incidents are related to each other or that any of them have anything to do with a malign actor, with a cybercriminal.”

#10644. Best Buy brings back 4K Blu-ray discs with a slowly expanding collection

It’s been nearly three years since Best Buy decided to ditch selling physical copies of movies, stripping its inventory from both its brick-and-mortar stores and its online catalog. But with the growing rise of 4K Blu-ray collections as a vinyl-esque collector’s item, the electronics giant has slowly begun dipping its toes back into the physical media game.

As spotted by TheHDRoom on Twitter and on the 4K Blu-ray subreddit, Best Buy is now selling 14 different 4K Blu-rays on its website, marking another step in the brand’s slow return to selling physical media. These films include:

Technically, Blu-ray returned to Best Buy’s online storefront in August of 2025, when it opened a third-party marketplace similar to Amazon or Walmart. These titles, however, are shipped and sold by Best Buy. Everything’s still online-only, unfortunately, save for The Odyssey, which launched in-store shelf displays alongside yesterday’s release date. It shouldn’t come as too much of a surprise that The Odyssey is getting some special treatment; in addition to its status as a box office juggernaut, Nolan’s previous movie, Oppenheimer, initially sold out of physical copies in its first week of release in 2023.

Outside of these 14 titles, additional Blu-rays continue to be sold through Best Buy’s third-party marketplace.

The company said it 'values its interactions with gamers above all else'

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

AMD Japan's X account has been met with sharp reproach after claiming the company loves gamers and values its interactions with gamers above all else, with commenters and even a community note expressing dissent at the sentiment, owing to AMD's comments on its "data center-first" ethos and a commitment to AI that has driven the price of consumer PC gaming up to nearly unaffordable levels.

Quoting a Tom's Hardware post on the X social network discussing CEO Dr. Lisa Su meeting gamers and autographing a GPU, the AMD Japan account was quick to seize the moment. "AMD values its interactions with gamers above all else," the post read, but it was met with a stark reminder that gamers don't feel the same way. The post soon received a new community note that pointed out that AMD's own CEO doesn't agree. In fact, Su was quoted as saying that AMD is now "a data center-first company," with the note also pointing to the fact that AMD's data center revenue increased 97% year-over-year, as per its Q3 2026 earnings report. That equates to around $8.5 billion in revenue, while client and gaming revenue fell 8% to $3.7 billion. Gaming alone was down 40%.

At the time, Su said that AMD expected its server revenue to double year over year in 2027, with its data center AI revenue growing at an even faster rate. "As a result, we expect data center to be more than eighty percent of our total revenue in 2027 and to drive the majority of our revenue and earnings growth," the CEO said during the company's earnings call.

ゲーマーとの交流を何よりも大切にするAMD。 コミュニティと直接つながり、その声に耳を傾け続ける姿勢こそAMDの強みです。 AMD loves gamers. 🎮November 17, 2026

By comparison, the same call heard Su say that, in 2027, AMD was "planning for PC market unit shipments to decline by a double-digit percentage." With this all in mind, it's perhaps no surprise that the AMD Japan X account stirred such a strong response from gamers who feel that AMD has left them behind in search of a more lucrative data center market. AMD is certainly not alone in that regard. Nvidia, once the darling of the PC component enthusiast, is now focused on AI data center components instead. The company's high-performance AI chips are so capable that they're export-controlled, something that, among other reasons, will put former CEO Jensen Huang in front of a House Foreign Affairs panel in December.

As for AMD, its ill-timed, misjudged X post ended with "AMD loves gamers." Gamers would appear to disagree.

#10642. Trump, Xi and Putin hold rare summit but deliver no breakthroughs on nuclear risks

For U.S. President Donald Trump, the meeting with the leaders of two other nuclear powers—China’s Xi Jinping and Russia’s Vladimir Putin—was supposed to be a diplomatic triumph: It would serve as proof that Trump could negotiate with two of America’s toughest rivals.

Before the trio met Thursday in a villa in the Chinese tech hub of Shenzhen, the U.S. president sought to flatter both Xi and Putin as his friends. They were to discuss the wars in Ukraine and Iran and how to reduce the dangers posed by nuclear arsenals.

But after roughly 70 minutes behind closed doors, they agreed to a far narrower agenda. They would like to reform the United Nations, refine more fossil fuels, and curb the reach of the International Criminal Court.

What was billed as a historic trilateral meeting in the end looked like a fleeting photo op. Trump quickly left the villa and boarded Air Force One without saying a word to reporters about his time with Xi and Putin. Still, he hailed the visit as “Highly Consequential” on social media as he made his way back to Washington and announced he would hold a press conference on Friday at the White House.

The agreements stemming from the trilateral meeting were modest, but the rhetoric that came at the start of it was not.

“We are really making history,” Trump said. “The United States, Russia and China are uniquely positioned to discuss how to reduce the number of nuclear weapons on Earth and also to lessen the risk of those weapons being used.”

Xi earlier said the meeting would focus on “promoting world peace.” Putin, for his part, said the countries “hold joint responsibility for maintaining international peace.”

After the meeting, Xi called it positive, constructive and fruitful, while Putin described the trilateral talks as open and candid, calling the format “useful and substantive.”

“It is a serious collaborative work,” he said in response to a question about whether the image of the meeting itself mattered more than the substance.

The last nuclear arms pact between Russia and the United States, known as New START, expired in February, and Trump has said he wants “a new, improved and modernized” deal that includes China. But China has less motivation to accept limits given that its arsenal of more than 600 warheads is far smaller than the more than 5,000 warheads believed to be in each of the U.S. and Russian stockpiles.

Daryl Kimball, executive director of the Arms Control Association, said the meeting could at best start a process that would eventually lead to a sustained set of talks for reducing nuclear arsenals.

“We need more than diplomatic theater and photo opportunities between Xi, Putin and Trump,” Kimball said. “And one meeting will not likely produce dramatic breakthroughs.”

The leaders also talked about Iran and trade

The U.S. president earlier had a separate one-on-one with Xi, their third substantial meeting this year.

Trump said they had a shared interest in having oil flow through the Strait of Hormuz, a key waterway for oil shipments from the Middle East that was repeatedly shut because of attacks in the Iran war. Trump suggested he would push Xi to buy more agricultural products from America.

Xi said that relations between the U.S. and China have been “generally stable and improved” during Trump’s presidency. The official Chinese news agency Xinhua reported that Xi discussed Taiwan with Trump and said the two countries needed to improve communication on economic and trade issues.

Trump also added to his calendar a separate sit-down with Putin, marking the first time the leaders have met for talks since their discussions in Alaska last year that failed to broker a deal on Russia’s war in Ukraine.

“Wonderful things can come out of this — or at least start,” Trump said, calling Putin “my friend.”

Trump has frequently boasted of his rapport with Putin, as relations between the U.S. and Ukraine appear to have cratered recently. Trump rejected new Russia sanctions, while Kyiv was undermined by disruptions in satellite support for its drones during the war.

After their trilateral meeting, the three leaders took aim at the International Criminal Court for overstepping its jurisdiction, saying government officials should enjoy immunity from its prosecutions. Putin is wanted by the court, and Trump’s administration has imposed sanctions on ICC officials and the institution itself as part of a campaign to dismantle the tribunal over investigations into the United States and its allies, including Israel.

Trump boasts about his relationships, but the ties between Xi and Putin are stronger

The U.S. president’s ambitions going into the meeting recalled an era of great power politics when wealthier countries felt entitled to decide the fate of seemingly lesser nations. Trump was negotiating with China and Russia without the involvement of Ukraine—or allies like Germany, France, the United Kingdom, Japan, and South Korea that also have stakes in the outcome of any talks.

Trump has long believed his ability to create personal connections gives him a diplomatic edge. Xi made a symbolic gesture of friendship Wednesday by taking the unusual step of greeting Air Force One on the tarmac, and the two leaders turned their handshake into a one-armed hug.

But for all the compliments the U.S. president has showered on the two autocrats, he did not always appear to have the same deep relationship based on common interests that Xi and Putin share.

On Wednesday at the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit, Xi greeted Putin by calling him “my old friend.” The Russian leader responded by declaring Xi to be his “dear friend.” China has helped economically support Russia after its 2022 invasion of Ukraine, providing Putin with a financial lifeline after the U.S. and its allies imposed new sanctions.

By contrast, when Xi went to the White House for a state visit in September, he was far more restrained with Trump, saying they had built a “personal rapport and stayed in touch.” Trump and Xi have one more meeting planned this year, at the G20 summit in Florida in December.

There were also signs of friction Thursday in the U.S.-China relationship despite Trump’s praise of Xi.

Chinese officials blocked a Bloomberg reporter from the trilateral meeting after she asked a question at the earlier Trump-Xi session. And Chinese television showed a video of Trump appearing drowsy, while the White House posted a clip of Trump helping Xi up an escalator.

—Josh Boak, Michelle L. Price, and Eduardo Castillo, Associated Press

Associated Press writers Darlene Superville, Didi Tang, Will Weissert, Huizhong Wu, and Kanis Leung contributed to this report.

#10640. Measles is back, and it’s a dire warning against shrinking the U.S. government

Sometimes, you really don’t know what you’ve got ‘til it’s gone. Take, for instance, the United States’ measles elimination status. Formally rescinded this week, after a 26-year run, the Pan American Health Organization’s new determination is about to make a lot of people realize what a quiet miracle it was to never have to think about measles.

Unfortunately, this genre of realization stands to become a lot more common. The official return of measles is just the starkest domestic example yet of the consequences that come with being led by an administration pot-committed to shrinking the federal government.

The U.S. spent the last century building institutions that provided federal education programs, environmental regulations, consumer protections, and other services benefitting the public. Although these institutions didn’t always work perfectly, they generally either improved U.S. citizens’ lives or shielded them from harm that local governments weren’t equipped to manage on their own. And for at least the 45 years since Ronald Reagan famously declared government was not the solution to America’s problem, but rather the problem itself, tearing down these institutions has been a holy-grail goal for some conservatives.

The most ardent proponents of that goal saw in Trump 2 a glimmering opportunity to dismantle the administrative state, as outlined extensively in the Project 2025 agenda. With the architect of Project 2025, Russell Vought, installed as White House budget director, the last two years have seen the steady evaporation or reorientation of institutions like the Department of Education, the Consumer Financial Protections Bureau (CFPB), and of course, Health and Human Services (HHS), whose current incarnation helped usher in the measles resurgence.

That result may be a preview of what happens when we lose mechanisms that solve certain problems so thoroughly, people seem to forget they were ever problems in the first place. It’s proof that weakening public protections doesn’t remove the need for those protections, it just makes us much less ready to combat everything they were protecting us from.

How we got measles again

Hours before news broke about America’s measles elimination status, current head of HHS Robert Kennedy Jr used social media to respond to a different breaking news story. He posted on X an AI-generated image of himself mimicking the photo Amazon MGM used to promote Robert Pattinson as the new James Bond. In the image, a tuxedo-clad Kennedy is holding an enormous carrot instead of Bond’s gun, and a caption reads: “Food. Real food.”

It would be hard to find a more apt representation of Kennedy’s tenure as the agency’s leader—a ridiculous, self-aggrandizing folly that depicts Kennedy as laser-focused on improving the quality of Americans’ food while actively ignoring the measles catastrophe he helped create.

Vaccination rates had already been declining since the pandemic, when hesitancy and outright opposition spread virally through so-called wellness influencers and other bad actors. Instead of addressing this trend as the dire threat it was, Trump put in charge of HHS someone who has promoted anti-vaccine positions for decades. Within months, Kennedy was undermining the conventional wisdom around measles vaccinations in settings as public as the Dr. Phil TV show.

Even more damaging: Kennedy kicked off his time at HHS by cutting $11 billion in state and local public-health grants. Those cuts forced health departments across the country to reduce staff, scale back critical services and, yes, either trim down or close various vaccination programs. Suddenly, the wastewater inspections, outbreak response teams, and buses traveling to areas riven by vaccine hesitancy were cut off at the knees.

Amid Kennedy’s efforts to change the childhood vaccination schedule and avoid acknowledging measles deaths, the U.S. has now reported 5,412 confirmed cases through November 13, compared to 2,289 for all of 2025, along with 10 reported measles deaths so far this year, up from three last year. According to the Center for Disease Control, cases have now surfaced in 46 states as well as Washington DC.

This is exactly the sort of result HHS’s supposedly bloated budget existed to prevent.

Shrinkage

The same governing philosophy that helped send the U.S. back into the 1950s on measles has also led to downsizing in several other institutions over the past two years—with even more devastating consequences in some cases.

Shortly after Trump’s second inauguration last year, his White House functionally destroyed the United States Agency for International Development (USAID), the primary department coordinating foreign development and humanitarian assistance around the globe. Before January 2025, USAID had approximately 13,600 employees; by April, only 241 remained. Annual U.S. foreign aid disbursements fell by $26 billion throughout the year, and the White House canceled roughly $80.5 billion in USAID and State Department foreign aid awards.

Although Secretary of State Marco Rubio issued a waiver for lifesaving humanitarian assistance programs, he added a laundry list of politically charged exemptions. These included programs involving abortions, family planning, “gender or DEI ideology programs”, and transgender surgeries. Because HIV/AIDS prevention didn’t fall under that waiver, and thanks to severe cuts to the George W. Bush-created President’s Emergency Plan for AIDS Relief (PEPFAR), 1,700 HIV treatment sites have since closed. One-year estimates of the global death toll from the loss of USAID stand at around 781,000, and long-term projections are in the range of 14 million. 

Beyond the staggering human toll, there’s bound to be a less-obvious domestic cost from the justified erosion of goodwill and diplomacy wrought by this administration’s callous actions.

Stateside, the Trump White House has also taken an axe to the Federal Emergency Management Agency (FEMA), arguing that disaster relief should be shifted to individual states. Never mind the obvious risk of exhausting state budgets, creating uneven recovery patterns, and losing large-scale coordination for major crises–the prospect of weaning disaster-stricken Americans off the government teat was simply too attractive!

Through staffing reductions that reduced FEMA’s workforce by 17% last year, terminated grants that left billions in funding up in the air, and the addition of administrative hurdles, FEMA is now much weaker than it was before inauguration. That weakness has thankfully only reared its head in relatively mild ways thus far. No domestic disaster in Trump 2 has yet been substantially exacerbated by FEMA Lite. A lot of disaster fund requests in that time have been delayed or denied, however, and a disproportionate amount of them have been in Democrat-led states.

Meanwhile, slashing roughly 28% of the Treasury Department’s workforce has degraded taxpayer services; hollowing out the CFPB has cost Americans $15 billion in overdraft and credit card late fees alone; and gutting the Office for Civil Rights within the Department of Education has slowed or recklessly dismissed thousands of discrimination cases. 

Little by little, the value of having high-functioning federal infrastructure is starting to become harder for anyone not in deep denial to dismiss or ignore.

The toothpaste is out of the tube

Perhaps the worst part about Trump and his enablers diving whole-hog into dismantling the administrative state is that it won’t be rebuilt any time soon, if ever. It’s a whole lot easier to swing a wrecking ball, after all, than it is to mount an ambitious construction project (unless it’s a ballroom-sized monument to the president’s ego).

When experienced staff leave specialized jobs, they often leave a crucial knowledge gap in their wake. When working relationships with outside agencies and other countries are severed, it takes a lot of time and effort to forge new ones—especially if those previous partners now consider your team unreliable. And when research and data collection projects come to a stop because of lost grant money, they seldom start back up again.

Many of the problems Americans now face because of our weakened institutions, and the ones we may face in the near-future, cannot be resolved with the stroke of a pen from an incoming majority-Democrat government. For instance, Kennedy could be impeached on Jan. 4and billions in public-health grants restored the day after, and the U.S. would still be far away from where we stood in January 2025. An epidemiology professor at Johns Hopkins University recently estimated it will take at least five years to reclaim measles elimination status in the U.S.

Getting rid of measles required decades of unflashy work involving vaccination campaigns and other public-health initiatives. Getting rid of our elimination status took less than two years.

If we ever want to get it back, not only will we have to play the Uno Reverse Card on a lot of what’s happened in Trump 2, we’ll have to collectively let go of the myth that strong federal institutions are somehow the biggest problem in America, rather than a source for solutions.

#10639. Could Meta fix its glasses privacy issues with an LED redesign?

Meta launched its own-branded smart glasses this summer (read: with no Ray-Ban or Oakley branding), and they seem to have done well for a number of reasons. Some models are much cheaper than Ray-Ban-branded pairs, while other Meta glasses carry Kylie Jenner or LISA names, bringing in a unique note of fashion, but none of these models changed the look of the camera LED indicator on the front.

Meta has come under fire over the past two years as people have abused the company’s smart glasses, giving them the unfortunate term “perv glasses” on social media because some people disabled the camera LED, which is supposed to illuminate when the camera is recording. Meta has issued several different software patches to disable the glasses’ camera when the LED is tampered with, but many have begun to feel like having a camera on smart glasses just feels wrong after people have abused the technology.

Meta glasses product lead David Woodland posted a render of the Gentle Monster cateye style glasses in a now-deleted Tweet on X on November 17, simply saying “I plead the fifth.” Presumably, he was calling out how similar these glasses look to the Meta Starfire Kylie Jenner style, but we’ll likely never know now.

Samsung and Google’s Warby Parker and Gentle Monster glasses certainly resemble Meta’s glasses, but they also do something noticeably different: the camera LED doesn’t look like the camera on the other side.

So this begs the question: can Meta do anything to salvage the unsavory association its glasses now have attached to them? We covered this topic on episode 125 of our Pixelated podcast, and Woodland’s post further highlights the question. A camera LED that’s obviously different from the camera on the other side would likely prompt people to ask about your glasses, while Meta’s design is purposefully stealthy because of its symmetry.

Does this design difference help Samsung’s Gentle Monster/Warby Parker glasses feel more private, and would a similar redesign help Meta’s glasses? I think it would help a lot, but I also want to know what our readers think. What say you, folks?

#10637. Elon Musk claims he’s ‘not MAGA’ in the wake of the blue wave

Since President Donald Trump’s reelection in 2024, Elon Musk has been one of his biggest allies in Silicon Valley. The Tesla CEO has served as both Trump’s employee and his friend: He spearheaded the Trump administration’s Department of Government Efficiency, aka DOGE, and wasn’t shy to tell the press that he came to “love” Trump over the course of their work together.

But in an interview at this week’s annual DealBook Summit, Musk suddenly sang a different tune.

“I’m not MAGA,” Musk said. “I’m not sure what MAGA means, but if that means sort of blind adherence to a set of policies by the Republican Party, then I certainly don’t have that.”

Musk may claim not to know the full extent of what “MAGA” means, but in the lead-up to the 2024 presidential election, he was happy to embrace the term. Wearing an infamous black MAGA hat to one of Trump’s rallies at Madison Square Garden, Musk told the crowd that he’s “not just MAGA,” but “dark gothic MAGA.”

Now, though, after the country’s historic shift to the left in this month’s midterms, Musk is positioning himself as bipartisan. “I think there are positives and negatives in both parties,” he said.

Musk’s historic midterm spending

Musk’s recent actions make it hard to believe he doesn’t consider himself bound to the Republican Party. In the midterm elections, he became the largest individual political donor in U.S. history, giving $446 million to conservative PACs including his own America PAC, which Musk founded in 2024 to support Trump’s presidential campaign.

But according to Musk, that record-breaking spending wasn’t in direct support of Republicans, but in support of a politically balanced Congress. His goal—“which failed, by the way,” Musk joked—was for Republicans to hold 51 seats in the Senate in 2029. 

“I think we want to have at least one of either the presidency, the House, or the Senate be of the other party,” Musk said.

But the blue tsunami proves that Musk’s investment didn’t pay off. Now, he says he feels “pretty foolish about having spent so much money and not seeing much for it.” 

“Probably, if I could rewind the clock, I would have spent less money,” Musk added.

Musk’s donations included hefty sums in his home base of Texas, where two Republican candidates he backed, Gov. Greg Abbott and Texas Attorney General Ken Paxton, lost to Democrats Gina Hinojosa and James Talarico respectively.

But Musk is looking on the bright side, saying he believes that Hinojosa “is going to be quite moderate” as governor.

“Based on everything I’ve heard about her, I think things will be fine in Texas with Gina as governor,” he said. “I don’t think she’s a far-left governor.”

A blue wave in tech?

Musk isn’t the only tech titan to downplay their G.O.P. affiliations in the wake of the midterms. Last week, Palantir CEO Alex Karp claimed the software company’s products are “essentially progressive,” despite its contracts with the Trump administration, including creating surveillance technology for ICE.

“I’ve been a Democrat my whole life. I grew up in a far-left environment,” Karp said. “I know more about being a progressive than almost anybody on the planet, and I absolutely believe Palantir—all of our products are essentially progressive.”

Like Musk, Carp said his support of Republicans has been in service of political moderation, not a right-wing takeover.

“My giving is equal Democrat, Republican,” Karp said. “I can tell you, without going into things I’m not gonna disclose, I’m very balanced, and everyone in the political arena knows it. And I’ll tell you what—I’m also not the village idiot.”

#10634. DDR6 RAM shrinks by 44% while delivering 4 times more bandwidth than DDR5 — JEDEC previews 12.8 GT/s modules with up to 1 terabyte of capacity per slot

JEDEC puts next-gen DDR6 memory on a diet

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Today's best RAM will look huge compared to upcoming DDR6 memory. JEDEC has just released a preview of the DDR6 specification, confirming that next-generation memory modules will be 44% shorter than DDR5. That is only the physical aspect, though. From a performance perspective, DDR6 will debut at 12.8 GT/s, more than double DDR5's baseline transfer rate, while also quadrupling the memory bandwidth per memory module thanks to a new design.

JEDEC's DDR6 preview highlights improvements in various areas, including performance, capacity, reliability, and power efficiency. These advancements align with the upgrades DDR5 brought to the table when it launched.

One of the most notable differences is that DDR6 memory modules will measure just 74mm in length, 44% shorter than DDR5’s 133mm memory modules. This makes DDR6 only 6% longer than DDR5 SO-DIMM modules, which you commonly find in laptops and mini-PCs.

JEDEC has maintained the pin count for many years, sticking to 288 pins for DDR4 and DDR5. However, DDR6 may finally break that mould. Given the substantial reduction in length with DDR6 and the data bus width upgrade, it is reasonable to assume that DDR6 will have a completely different pinout and density. Sadly, JEDEC has not revealed the pin count for DDR6.

Early rumors suggested that DDR6 may be limited to CAMM form factors to feed the AI boom. However, JEDEC's announcement at least does not rule out other form factors. It appears JEDEC is still determining which form factors DDR6 will use. Potential options include RDIMM, UDIMM, and CAMM formats, just like DDR5.

Baseline JEDEC Bandwidth Per Module (GB/s)

Peak Baseline Bandwidth Per Module (GB/s)

Notably, JEDEC has increased the data bus width in DDR6 to 96 bits, a 50% boost over DDR5's 64 bits. The channel architecture for DDR6 will also change substantially. While DDR4 and DDR5 leveraged a single 64-bit channel and two 32-bit sub-channels, DDR6 takes a different approach. JEDEC will implement a design with four 24-bit sub-channels.

Using the baseline for comparison, DDR6 offers up to 153.6 GB/s of total module bandwidth, compared to DDR5's 38.4 GB/s. That is a 4X uplift in bandwidth. If we compare DDR5-9200 RDIMMs, which max out at 73.6 GB/s per module, with DDR6-12800 RDIMMs, the uplift is a little more than double.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

At least on the server side, DDR6 RDIMMs will start at DDR6-12800, whereas DDR5 RDIMMs topped out at DDR5-9200. JEDEC anticipates DDR6 reaching DDR6-14400 and beyond. This is for the server and enterprise market, though. It remains to be seen which DDR6 speed will launch in the consumer market. DDR5 commenced at DDR5-4800.

DDR6 may be shorter than DDR5, but it makes up for it by having 2X the bank count. This will help push higher data rates and capacities. DDR6 will support 128Gb (16GB) dies, which represents a massive leap. We are looking at 4X the density of early DDR5 memory modules. Instead of placing chips side by side on the PCB, DDR6 enables die stacking, allowing manufacturers to place multiple dies on top of each other via Through-Silicon Vias (TSVs).

Consumer DDR5 memory modules currently sit at 64GB per module, while server RDIMMs have reached 512GB. DDR6 could raise module capacity to 128GB or 256GB and potentially scale RDIMMs to 1TB or more per slot.

DDR6 has no official launch date. Many believe 2029 is a realistic target for commercialization. However, in the current state of things, it would be a miracle if DDR6 had no setbacks.

#10632. Find Orphan Pages by Comparing Your Sitemap with an Internal Crawl

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A page can return 200 OK, exist in an XML sitemap, and still be practically invisible to anyone browsing the site.

You do not need a full commercial crawler to find the obvious cases. A small Node.js script can compare two things:

Anything present in the first set but missing from the second deserves investigation.

You now have URLs that the application publishes through its sitemap but that your HTML crawl could not reach.

The page might be intentionally isolated. It might exist for a campaign. Navigation might depend on JavaScript. Your crawler may have missed authentication or pagination.

Should a normal visitor reasonably be able to discover this page from another page on the site?

Google recommends using standard crawlable elements with resolvable href values for links that should be discoverable. Google's guidance on crawlable links

That is also good web architecture independent of SEO.

Internal links should not exist merely to move ranking signals around.

They should help users move between related tasks.

A technical guide might lead to a product page. A product page might link back to the configuration guide. A category page should make important child resources discoverable without requiring a user to know the exact URL.

This is why on-page SEO and internal linking are closely connected to site architecture. Good internal links clarify relationships for both visitors and crawlers.

At a broader level, crawlability is one part of technical SEO and search visibility. A page that nobody can reliably discover is difficult to evaluate regardless of how strong its content may be.

If an important URL appears disconnected because navigation is created only after client-side JavaScript runs, the script has surfaced a real architectural fact.

You can then decide whether that is acceptable.

If your application genuinely requires rendered-DOM crawling, replace the fetch-and-Cheerio layer with Playwright.

Do not make the simple crawler pretend to be something it is not.

The best output from this tool is not an "SEO score."

It is a concrete question for the development team:

This page exists. The sitemap knows about it. Why can't a normal HTML crawl reach it?

#10627. Power BI MCP server: how to brief an AI agent to build accurate measures

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Power BI, Microsoft's reporting and dashboard tool, now lets an AI agent work on the data model behind a report. If you ask one to add a year-on-year revenue measure (a measure is Power BI's name for a saved calculation) with Microsoft's MCP server connected, it can write the formula and save it to the model without you opening the formula bar. When we tried this on a sample model, an agent inspected the model, wrote the measure, tested it and saved it in under four minutes.

The agent did a careful job, but its formula didn't follow the same rules the finance team uses to count revenue. Nobody had told it what "revenue" meant in this business, so it had to decide for itself. We then gave another agent a short brief that set out finance's rules for what counts as revenue and which date to count it by. The agent wrote the formula from those rules, and the result matched finance's figure exactly. The two results show how much difference it makes in how you brief an AI agent to build accurate measures.

We wanted to find out what an agent can see in a Power BI model and how to brief it so the measure matches the business's own definitions, with the setup arranged so that every change can be checked. Developers will know the principle from agents that write SQL or API code, where the brief ends up doing the job of a specification.

Because MCP (Model Context Protocol) is an open standard, the same server can be used from different AI clients, GitHub Copilot in VS Code and Claude among them, although some clients need extra sign-in setup for the hosted version.

Power BI has two current MCP servers, plus an older one, the Power BI Consumption MCP server, kept for existing integrations. The first of the current pair is the Power BI Authoring MCP server, which you may still see under the name of its package, powerbi-modeling-mcp. It lets an agent create and change the objects inside a semantic model, which is the layer of a Power BI report that holds the tables, the relationships between them and the calculations, kept separate from the charts on the page. It comes in two forms. The hosted version, still in preview, needs nothing installed. The local version is generally available and runs on your own machine, which makes it the one to use when the model is a file you hold yourself, whether that's open in Power BI Desktop, the free Windows app used to build reports, or saved as a project folder. Macs aren't supported by the local version, so on a Mac it has to be the hosted one, which works only with models published to Microsoft's cloud (a Fabric workspace).

The second is the Fabric IQ MCP server. Fabric IQ is part of Microsoft Fabric, the wider data platform that Power BI now sits within. It gives an agent read-only tools for finding published reports and models and running queries against them, within the permissions already set, so the agent can answer questions about the data but can't change a model.

Which one to use follows from the job. Anything that changes a model goes through the authoring server, whether that's a new measure, a relationship or a set of descriptions, and it has to be the local version if the model is a Desktop file or a project folder under source control. Fabric IQ is for agents that only need answers from models other people maintain.

An agent connected to a semantic model can see its metadata (table and column names, data types, relationships, existing measures and any descriptions someone has written) and can run queries against the data. Everything it produces is inferred from those clues. A model that has served a business for years carries names that made perfect sense to the people who built it, and a column called Amt on a table called Sales_v2 tells an agent far less than it tells them.

Names carry a lot of the meaning. A column called Amount could hold the value with VAT (the UK's sales tax) or without, before credit notes or after, and an agent asked for "revenue" has to pick one. If the model holds several revenue measures built for different reports, the agent has to choose between them, and the one it picks may have been built for a different purpose.

The formulas depend on context as well. Power BI measures use a syntax of their own called DAX, which stands for Data Analysis Expressions. A DAX result shifts with whatever the person viewing the report has filtered or clicked on, which is known as filter context. A developer might think of a measure as a function that reads implicit state set by its caller. The same measure can be right as a single total on a card and need different handling once it's split by product in a table or filtered by a date slicer (the on-page date filter), depending on how CALCULATE (the DAX function that changes which filters apply) is used. Which behaviour is correct depends on how the business wants the number to respond, and that's something the agent has to be told.

Relationships work the same way. Plenty of models have more than one date column on the sales table, say order date and dispatch date, with only one relationship active at a time. A DAX measure uses the active relationship unless it's written to switch to the other one, and the agent has no way of knowing which date your monthly reporting runs on unless something tells it.

In each case the agent can write a valid measure. Which of two valid measures matches the business's own definition is the part it can't see from the model.

To see this in practice, we built a small sales model in a Power BI Desktop file, working through the server itself, with invented data: 5,266 order lines, with order dates from January 2025 to October 2026. We gave the Sales table an Amt column holding each line's value after discount with 20% VAT added, next to LineTotal and Discount columns that excluded VAT, and an existing [Revenue] measure that summed Amt. There were two date columns, with the active relationship to the Date table on dispatch date and the order date relationship inactive. Orders stop at the end of October 2026, which made the current year a part year, a deliberate test. Nothing in the model had a description. In this made-up business, finance reports revenue net of VAT and discounts, by order date.

On 12 November 2026 we connected an AI agent (Anthropic's Claude Fable 5.2) to version 1.0.1 of the local server through a small Node.js script that speaks MCP over stdio. In all three runs the agent had the server's tools only, without Microsoft's powerbi-authoring plugin, which adds modelling guidance on top of them. It was also told that nobody was available to answer questions, so each measure was saved exactly as the agent wrote it, with no review in between. The first prompt was one sentence: "Add a year-on-year revenue measure."

Before writing anything, the agent listed the tables, relationships and columns, read the existing measure, sampled some rows and checked the date ranges. It worked out for itself that 2026 was a part year and limited the comparison to the same dates in 2025, which avoided a misleading result of -6.7%. From 15 sample rows it also spotted that Amt looked like the net figure plus 20%.

The measure it saved reused the existing [Revenue] measure and followed the active relationship. This is the core of it, with the date handling and blank checks left out. CurrentDates is defined in those lines as the dates up to the last order:

It returned growth of 21.5% for 2026, which is correct for revenue counted by dispatch date. On finance's definition, by order date, the answer is 18.6%, and the whole difference came from that choice of date. The VAT question made no difference to a percentage, because Amt was already net of discount and every line carried the same rate, although it would have overstated any figure in pounds by 20%.

We reset the model and repeated the run with a fresh agent, which made the same two choices and returned the same 21.5%. Both choices were reasonable readings of the model as it stood, and the DAX was sound. In their reports, both agents flagged the date basis and the VAT treatment as open questions, and both worked out that an order-date basis would give 18.6%. That's what a good analyst would do, and with those two answers they would have had everything they needed.

Think of a capable analyst on their first morning, someone who knows DAX well but has never seen your business before and knows nothing (or not a lot) about it. You wouldn't say "add a revenue measure" and walk off. You'd tell them which table holds the trusted figures, whether revenue includes VAT, which date drives the reporting calendar, and what they shouldn't touch. An agent needs that briefing for the same reasons, and getting good work out of it draws on the skills you'd use with any new starter. Developers do the same thing when they write a good ticket. A request for a measure is a requirement, and it needs the same definitions and constraints, plus a way to check the result.

Before you send a prompt, ask yourself whether you could do the task properly if you were given only the words in that prompt and the model itself. If the answer is no, then the agent is in the same position, and it will make its own assumptions.

For the detailed run we cleared the earlier measure from the model and gave a fresh agent this brief:

This time the measure summed LineTotal minus Discount and used USERELATIONSHIP, the DAX function that switches a calculation to an inactive relationship, to work from order date. It returned 18.6% from that one brief, with no follow-up prompt, which is the figure we had calculated separately from the source data. This is the full measure as the agent saved it:

Each line of the brief answered a question the agent would otherwise have had to settle alone. It also made some sensible decisions the brief hadn't covered. It left the grand total blank, for instance, where a plain total across both years would have shown a meaningless growth figure of 151.2%. The final line of the brief, asking to see the DAX before anything is saved, is there so that a person can see choices like that. In the test we approved that step in advance, to keep the run unattended.

One detail needs adjusting before this measure is reused on other data. Its guard checks that each comparison period starts on or after the first order date, which works here because the sample data has an order on 1 January 2025. When we swapped that date for 2 January 2025 in a copy of the measure, January 2026 and the 2026 row came back blank. Comparing against the first date in the Date table, as the first agent's measure did, brought the 2026 figure back, since the Date table starts on 1 January. That version doesn't confirm that sales exist for the whole comparison period, though, so the right check depends on whether the business wants growth shown only where a complete prior period exists. That's a decision for whoever reviews the measure.

We ran the one-sentence prompt twice and the detailed brief once, all with the same AI model and one small set of data, which is enough to illustrate the pattern but too little to say how often it happens.

Good briefs help, but you'll be writing the same definitions into prompts again and again unless they live in the model itself. Descriptions on tables, columns and measures are part of the metadata the agent reads, so filling them in is one of the cheapest ways to put business definitions in front of it every time. In our test, where nothing had a description, both agents given the one-sentence prompt noted that nothing confirmed what Amt contained. A description such as "Net sales value in GBP, excluding VAT, after credit notes" stays in the model, where the agent reads it without anyone retyping it.

Naming deserves the same attention. Spelling out abbreviated column names, hiding technical keys that no report needs, gathering measures into a single measures table, and removing measures that are no longer used all give the agent fewer things to choose between. These are the same habits that make a model easier for a new colleague to pick up, so asking someone outside the team to find a figure in it is a fair test of the result.

Microsoft has also added a set of Prep data for AI features (in preview) to Power BI Desktop and the service. They let you choose which fields Copilot, Microsoft's AI assistant, should consider and write AI instructions that explain your business terms, with verified answers available for common questions. They're aimed at Copilot, but deciding and writing down your definitions is the same work whichever AI tool reads them.

An agent working through the authoring server has the same access as the person signed in, so anything that person can change, the agent can change. Sign in with an account that has no more access than the job needs, and take a copy of the model first, as you would before any bulk edit.

Start with read-only mode. The local server takes a --readonly flag that blocks all write operations, which is the right setting while you're learning how the agent behaves on your models. When we tried to create a measure in this mode, the server refused and said it was running read-only. In VS Code, the configuration looks like this (other clients use a similar structure, sometimes under an mcpServers key instead of servers):

You'll need Node.js installed for the npx command. The server won't run other tools until its licence terms (the EULA) have been accepted. Once you've read them, you can either tell the agent to accept for you or add --accepteula to the args list.

The --readonly flag belongs to the local server. On a Mac the route is the hosted server and a copy of the model in a Fabric workspace, once a Fabric administrator has switched on the tenant setting that allows the MCP endpoint, and there the copy is the safeguard. For an enforced limit, sign in with an account that holds only Build permission on the model, which lets the agent read and query it and nothing more.

Read-write is the default, so check how confirmation behaves in the version you install. Version 1.0.1 skips confirmation prompts by default, which the project's README hasn't caught up with, and has a --require-confirmation flag to turn them on. The flag is listed in the server's own --help output. In our runs every edit went through without a prompt. When we added the flag to the args list, the server asked for confirmation before the first write, with a message beginning "Are you sure you want to perform operations that will modify your database", and answering no blocked the change.

Version control is the other safeguard. Saved in the Power BI Project format (PBIP), a report and its model become folders of text files instead of one binary .pbix file. Commit those before the agent starts and its work arrives as an ordinary diff, which you can review and revert like any other change.

Whatever the agent reads, including table and column names and the rows returned by any query, is passed on to the AI provider behind your client, which may sit outside Microsoft's compliance commitments. For a model holding customer or payroll data, check that the provider is approved under your data policies before you connect.

An agent can draft and test a measure in minutes, and someone still has to read what comes back. For a developer new to Power BI, that takes enough DAX to see what a measure's filters will do in a table broken down by month or product, and enough knowledge of the business to confirm the agent used the right column and the right date. Writing the brief takes the same knowledge. Ours could only have come from someone who knew the model had an inactive relationship and which columns carried VAT.

These are data modelling and DAX skills, which many learners find the hardest part of Power BI. If the person reviewing agent output hasn't had that grounding yet, it helps to know how to judge whether a Power BI course covers enough DAX and data modelling, because courses vary a great deal in how much time they give to those two subjects compared with building charts.

Take a copy of a model you know well and connect the local server to it in read-only mode. Ask the agent to describe every table and measure in its own words, then check those descriptions against what you know the model does. Wherever the two differ, the model needs a clearer name or a written description, and the corrected text can go straight into the description fields. With those in place before you allow any writes, the agent starts its first real task with the same definitions your finance team uses.

#10625. Critical Java vulnerability puts millions of sites, services, and devices at risk of remote code execution
