Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

The Winds of Winter have arrived — just not written by G.R.R. Martin.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

We've just had a few high-stakes security vulnerabilities making systems administrators run around with their heads on fire, and the deluge doesn't look like it's stopping any time soon. Java, the programming language that Oracle has always claimed is on billions of devices, has a critical security vulnerability that allows for remote code execution (RCE), often without even needing to log in to the application. The consequence is that an attacker can gain access to the target system, read user and internal data, and have a launch point for further shenanigans, including database access and potential system takeover.

The flaw was dubbed Java4Shell by Calif Research, the lab that found the vulnerability. The researchers say it rates 9.2 on the newer CVSS 4.0 scale, though individual reports from companies like Oracle itself and Red Hat give it their own ratings, but they're all in the 8 to 9.8 range depending on the vendor. The root cause is a bug in OpenJDK, the library that nearly every distribution of Java, whether Oracle's or anyone else's, derives their implementations from.

You can see an exploit of the bug in action below across multiple software services.

The actual bug is a flaw in an optimization for Base64 decoding, functionality that's ubiquitous in any programming language. Base64 is a format to allow for easy transformation of binary data into plain text. In the modern day, it's used to embed files in the middle of text, like inserting image data in the middle of HTML or CSS, attaching files to emails (internally they're all text-based), or simply representing data as text for visual inspection, like file checksums or cryptographic key fingerprints.

Oracle has already fixed the bug and issued updated versions of its wares. However, much like digital certificates, operating systems and even individual programs include their own copy of a Java executable, meaning that the potential blast radius is far wider than you'd expect at first glance. Calif listed many such common applications: at the time of the post, Keycloak, Jira, Confluence, Jenkins, and PingFederate all had full unauthenticated RCE, while Apache Kafka Broker, Solr, Elasticsearch, Gerrit, and Spinnaker have RCEs after authentication.

Because Java is so darn widespread, most every company has to issue security advisories and updates for their wares, including IBM, Red Hat, Siemens, and Broadcom. Because so many containers also use Java-based applications in some form, those too are vulnerable, and the count of individual vulnerable container images across registries everywhere is likely a high figure. This is likely not a bug that will be easily eradicated.

There is a condition to trigger the exploit, but it's not too difficult to fulfill. The Java instance in question must be running on an x86-64 CPU with AVX-512 support and the VBMI extension. In practice, that means chips from the Intel Ice Lake and AMD Zen 4 families, from 2021 and 2022 respectively, for their server variants. Older CPUs are not affected, as the code optimization that contains the bug is specific to VBMI.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

The specific bug is in a hand-written assembly optimization that fails a simple bounds check. If an attacker inputs 64 As and an "=" sign as padding, they can overwrite neighboring memory, and then weaponize that corruption, similar to how Heartbleed worked. As usual these days, Calif says that the vulnerability was found by an AI bot, with $200 worth of tokens. Though unlike many other examples, this one was actually fairly deep, and it wasn't low-hanging fruit that illustrated poor code or network security hygiene.

He's obsessed with detail and has a tendency to ramble on the topics he loves. When not doing that, he's usually playing games, or at live music shows and festivals.

#10620. Fitbit Edge after 30 days: Life in the middle lane [Video]

The biggest compliment you can pay Google at the moment is that alongside the Pixel Watch, recent Fitbit releases have been a huge return to form – the Fitbit Edge included.

I need to nail my colors to the mast; I’m a Fitbit Air fanboy. I’ve talked about it multiple times on the Pixelated podcast, here on the site, and it made me switch away from the Pixel Watch 5 because of how easily it fit into my existence without insisting on itself.

The Fitbit Edge worried me at first because it comes with a screen, which is something I realized isn’t that important to me day-to-day. After 30 days of wear, I’m conflicted because maybe I was too hasty to ditch a display on my wrist.

The design is somewhat in a halfway house because it feels like it’s trying to be both a classic Fitbit while integrating some of the Pixel Watch features we know and love. Initially, I loved it, but it has become something that isn’t quite enough in either direction to suit my own tastes.

My love for the simplicity of the Fitbit Air has colored how I feel, but the Edge has an excellent core design. The display bezels remind me heavily of the original Pixel Watch; yes, they’re there, but you only notice them when the light catches the frame edge.

After a month of daily wear, the display has lost a bit of its luster. The oleophobic coating is just about holding up, but now we’re into the cooler months; it’ll be put through a barrage of jacket cuffs and hoodie sleeves. This is one of the only durability concerns I have had so far.

Despite that admittedly minor concern, the Fitbit Edge is not something you need to baby. I’ve bashed it with weights, caught my wrist on zips (zippers for you, my American friends), and bashed it around with reckless abandon. It’s solid and hasn’t picked up as much as a single scuff. I don’t care too much if a fitness tracker gets a beating, whereas I would be more concerned about damaging my Pixel Watch.

If you come in wanting a smartwatch-like experience from the Fitbit Edge, I think you’ll instantly be frustrated by the UI. It can feel cramped when you see notifications, and the large bezels don’t help all that much, but Google has well optimized almost all of the key sections outside of this.

Tiles are very well implemented; they’re just a smaller, squatter take on the Pixel Watch tile system, and it’s one of the best things about the Fitbit Edge interface. It’s also really snappy, which is something that isn’t the case with cheaper fitness trackers such as this. I’m constantly checking the weather or using the Google Health tile, but I have loved the quick access complication on the default watch face.

I tried a few of the other designs over the past 30 days; a lot of them are great. The default has become my favorite. It’s information-dense without being too overloaded. Tapping the complications takes you to the exact data point. I genuinely love how fuss-free an experience it is, save for a few minor complaints.

We could criticize the accuracy of the data or the lack of tracking for certain types of workouts, but I think that misses the point of the Fitbit Edge. It’s a simple tracker with a screen, and the fact that you get even basic ECG on a device of this size is impressive.

I truly have worn the Edge daily for a full month, and while the bands are comfortable, I have found that they cause me to get a rash on the underside of my left wrist just under the palm. It’s actively stopping me from wearing it all the time now. I hope that the new connector doesn’t slow down third-party development for bands, because I’m desperate for a fabric band to alleviate this issue.

This is exacerbated when the rubber band gets wet or sweaty. If you have sensitive skin, you might have the same problem as I have. Using some moisturizer has helped, but it is something I wasn’t prepared to suffer with before the Fitbit Edge turned up. I get that this is likely a “me” problem, but I wanted to note it.

When the screen gets wet or moist, it can bug out quite a lot. Taps don’t register properly, and a few water droplets can activate all the menus and submenus. Luckily, it won’t force your phone to place a call or anything like that, but the water protection mode is a must. I have kept it on extensively.

Swimmers will probably get super frustrated; heck, I was on a rainy day as the screen kept bugging out. Overall, though, I have enjoyed the fitness tracking. Unlike on the Fitbit Air, it is nice to have a screen to start a workout rather than head over to my phone. That said, the experience is the same as on your phone, but scaled down to fit the watch face.

I dislike that you don’t get a phone notification to show the current workout progress by default, like the Fitbit Air. I had become accustomed to that. Luckily, you can enable it in the Google Health settings. I like having this as I forget to stop workouts so often without a prompt to hit “stop.”

With audio playback, you’re also getting the partial benefits of watch-phone controls, but it’s a two-step process unlike on Pixel Watch. You either have to go to the “Media” app or have the tile active and navigate to it to access play/pause and all your other streaming app controls. I tend not to bother using these as it’s finicky.

This had me thinking about NFC. I think a lot of people would have liked NFC for Google Wallet on the Fitbit Edge, but I think having to enter a PIN on such a small screen would really have been a nightmare. I’ve made wireless payments with my watch less than five times, as it’s just easier to use my phone rather than contort my wrist to a wireless payment terminal. I figure this was something that was thought about and makes a lot of sense given the constraints.

One of my other complaints is that the DND and Bedtime modes from my account haven’t synced or at least don’t appear to sync. Unless I’m mistaken, you need to activate these settings manually every time, which has been super annoying when in bed, as a flick of your wrist can blind you in a dark room. I figure this is something that can be fixed with an update, so here’s hoping.

I’ve been super impressed with the battery life. A week is easily attainable for me with up to three gym visits per week, so I’ve charged 5 times in a month. That, to me, is excellent. I picked up a cheap charging dock, as the Edge uses the same charger for the Fitbit Air, so I figured why not.

It’s not the best-looking dock, but I quite like how it airs off my Edge so it’s not as stinky after gym sessions – as I wipe with an alcohol pad and clean as regularly as possible.

The experience is fine; it’s almost a third of the price of the Pixel Watch and lacks a few features due to the various compromises – compromises I can deal with, as smartwatches have become information overload for me over time, and I like that the app selection is ultra-refined. At no point does the Fitbit Edge give you information overload.

Coming from a Charge 6 or an older Fitbit, this is an amazing replacement. It’s the new “default” fitness tracker for people who want all the hallmarks of what makes a great Fitbit.

There is just enough. Nothing more, nothing less. To me, this feels like the perfect middle ground for a lot of people. You want something simple, touchscreen-enabled, and that doesn’t get in the way. As impressed as I have been with the Edge, though, I’ve loved a fuss-free tech accessory so much recently that I’ll probably go back to the Fitbit Air.

#10619. What is Model Routing in AI?

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

"Do you know the closest ATM?" and "I was charged twice, the refund never arrived, and now my card is blocked" are both customer messages. Most AI apps send both to the same large model and pay large-model prices for each one.

Model routing fixes that. A router looks at each request and sends it to the cheapest model that can handle it, and keeps the expensive model for the requests that need it.

In this guide we'll cover what model routing is, how routers decide, and what it saves. Then we'll build a small router in about 60 lines of JavaScript and run it over 200 customer messages. At one setting it matched a large model's accuracy for about a third of the cost. At another it came within a point and a half for 89% less.

Model routing in AI is the practice of sending each request to the model best suited to it, instead of sending every request to one model. A router sits between your app and a pool of models, reads each request, and picks a model based on what you care about: cost, speed, or quality.

You'll also see it called AI model routing, LLM routing, or LLM model routing. They all mean the same thing. The pool might be a small and a large model from one provider, or models from several providers behind one API.

The idea rests on one observation: most requests are easy. A small model answers them as well as a large one, in a fraction of the time and for a fraction of the price. The router's whole job is finding the hard ones.

The diagram below shows a typical AI model routing architecture.

Model routing works in three steps: the router reads the request, decides which model should answer it, and forwards the request to that model. The middle step is where routers differ. Routing tools decide which model to use in one of four ways.

Rule-based routing uses rules you write by hand. Requests from free-plan users go to a small model. Anything tagged as code goes to a coding model. Prompts over a certain length go to a model with a bigger context window. It's simple, predictable, and free to run, but the rules only know what you thought of in advance.

Intelligent model routing uses a model to make the decision. A small, fast classifier reads each request and predicts which model in the pool will answer it well. You'll also see it called smart or dynamic model routing.

Two more terms come up here. Task-aware routing picks a model by the kind of job, like summarising, coding, or answering a support question. Context-aware routing also looks at what surrounds the request, like the conversation so far, the user, or the tools available.

The big platforms work this way. Microsoft describes its Foundry model router as "a trained language model" that picks a model for each prompt in real time. Google says Gemini Notebook uses its new Decisions API as a real-time router, serving everyday queries with up to 40% lower latency. I've tested the Google Decisions API against Jev on the same messages we'll use below.

Cascade routing tries the cheapest model first and only moves up when the answer isn't good enough. The usual test is confidence: if the cheap model is sure, keep its answer. If it isn't, send the request to a stronger model. It's also called confidence-based routing, and it's what we'll build below.

A cascade needs no separate classifier, because the first model's own confidence does the routing. The trade-off is that escalated requests pay for two models and wait for both.

Fallback routing is about staying up, not saving money. If a model times out, hits a rate limit, or its provider has an outage, the router sends the request to the next model on a list. Most production routers combine fallbacks with one of the three methods above.

The main benefits of model routing in AI systems are lower cost, faster responses, and fewer outages, without giving up quality on the hard requests.

We'll build a cascade router for a job every support tool has: sorting customer messages. Each message gets one of ten labels, like atm support or request refund.

The test set is 200 messages from Banking77, a public set of banking support messages: ten labels, 20 messages each. They're the same 200 messages from my zero shot classification test, which is where the single-model numbers below come from.

Put both keys in a .env file and run the examples with node --env-file=.env. It's the same setup as in my guide to getting a YouTube API key.

Create router.mjs. The first function sends the message to Jev with one multiple-choice question, and returns the label Jev picked along with its confidence:

Confidence runs from 0 to 1. When nearly all the probability sits on one label, it's close to 1. When Jev is torn between two labels, it drops.

The second function asks Claude Opus 5.5 the same question. The output_config block limits the answer to one of our labels, so there's no free text to parse. SitePoint's guide to LLM structured output with JSON Schema explains how that works.

The router itself is about a dozen lines:

If Jev is at least 80% confident, its answer goes straight back. If not, Claude answers instead. And if Claude fails, the router falls back to Jev's answer, because a shaky answer beats an error page.

Try it on an easy message and a harder one:

Jev was sure about the lost card. On the ATM question it leaned towards card acceptance with a confidence of 0.64, so the router asked Claude, which got it right.

Because cheap, strong, and threshold are options, the same function routes between any two models.

Model routing reduces AI costs by keeping easy requests on a cheap model and paying for the expensive one only when it's needed. Here's what that looked like on our 200 messages, starting with each model on its own:

Opus gets six more messages right out of 200, and costs more than 120 times as much.

Now the router, at four confidence thresholds:

Speed improved too. At the 0.8 threshold, the messages Jev kept came back in a median of 227 ms. The ones that escalated took about 2.1 seconds, because they waited for both models.

A note on method: I ran every message through both models once and saved the answers, then replayed the router over them at each threshold. That's how you compare thresholds without paying for the same test four times.

Then I ran the router live to check. At 0.95 it scored the same 95.0% and sent 61 messages to Opus. At 0.8 it scored 93.5% and sent 20, a point under the replay. Two answers moved: one of Jev's confidence scores landed at 0.82 instead of 0.78, just over the line, and Opus changed its answer on one message. Scores near the threshold shift a little between runs, so leave yourself a margin.

The choice of cheap model matters less than you'd think. With Google's Gemini Decision 1 in first place instead of Jev, a 0.7 threshold reached the same 95% for US$0.28 per 1,000 messages.

Model routing can cost a little quality, and the threshold decides how much. In our test the gap to Opus ranged from nothing at 0.95 to two points at 0.7.

The loss comes from one place: answers the cheap model got wrong while feeling sure. At the 0.8 threshold, Jev kept five wrong answers because its confidence was above the line. Opus got four of those five wrong as well, so only one was a real loss: "Do all ATMs take this card?", which Jev filed under card acceptance with a confidence of 0.91.

Escalating isn't risk-free either. On one message, Jev had the right label with a confidence of 0.44, and Opus overruled it with the wrong one.

To optimize costs using AI model routing, measure first, route second, and keep measuring. These six steps cover LLM model routing cost optimization for any pair of models:

The return is easy to estimate once you know the split. If nine in ten requests stay on a model that costs 1% as much, the bill drops by about 89%, which is what we measured.

Several inference providers have multi-model routing built in, and most AI gateway platforms support model routing too, so you don't have to write your own. As of November 2026, these are the top model routing solutions for AI apps. Each name links to the tool's model routing docs:

Hosted model routing platforms for large language models decide for you, which is the fastest way to start. A hand-built router like ours takes more work, but you choose the signal and you can see why every request went where it did.

An LLM router is the component that does model routing for large language models. It can be a function in your own code, like the one in this guide, a hosted model such as Microsoft Foundry's model router, or a feature of an AI gateway.

Smart model routing is another name for intelligent model routing: a model, not a hand-written rule, decides where each request goes. Dynamic model routing means the same thing.

The ROI of AI model routing depends on how much of your traffic a cheaper model can handle. In our test, a 0.8 threshold kept 89.5% of messages on the cheap model and cut the cost per 1,000 messages from US$1.96 to US$0.22, for about a point of accuracy. The build was about 60 lines of code.

OpenRouter's auto model routing works through its Auto Router. You set the model to openrouter/auto, and a decision model picks a model and a reasoning effort for each request. You pay the standard rate of whichever model it selects, with no extra fee.

OpenClaw model routing is a primary model plus an ordered list of fallbacks. In the OpenClaw model routing configuration you set agents.defaults.model.primary and agents.defaults.model.fallbacks, and an agent can override the fallback list. When a model fails, OpenClaw first rotates auth profiles on the same provider, then moves to the next model on the list, as its model failover docs describe. That makes it fallback routing, not cost-based routing.

An AI gateway simplifies model routing by putting every model behind one API, one set of keys, and one place for logs. Your app calls the gateway, and the routing rules live in config instead of code, so changing a model doesn't need a deploy.

Mid-stream failover is when a router switches models after a streamed response has already started, because the first model failed partway through. It's the hardest kind of failover, because the user has already seen part of an answer. Many routers only fail over before the first token arrives and return an error after that, so check what your gateway does.

#10615. Vivo X500 Pro camera review: Battle of the small(er) phones vs Pixel 11 Pro

The compact Vivo X500 Pro has some camera downgrades compared to the Pro Max, so are you better off saving money and getting the base Vivo X500 instead?

Over the past few years, Vivo has made a name for itself as the camera powerhouse smartphone company. The first time I used a Vivo X100 Pro blew my mind in a way smartphone cameras hadn’t for a few years, but does the company’s 4th iteration — yes, Vivo skipped the X400 — of the X series still impress?

The answer is mostly yes. The Vivo X500 series includes five models: the base X500, the X500 Pro, and the X500 Pro Max. Unlike some other phones, all three of these models feature different camera hardware that takes advantage of the physical size differences. The X500 Pro Max’s hardware is the best of the bunch, but that phone is substantially larger and heavier as a result.

So I’m comparing the more reasonably sized models to see which semi-compact Vivo phone you should consider this year. I tossed the Google Pixel 11 Pro into the comparison since Google also offers a compact flagship camera phone.

Most camera specs between these phones are surprisingly comparable, so the differences you’re about to see are largely caused by some pretty massive differences in processing. I already covered Vivo’s wild new AI camera mode, so let’s go for something a little closer to normal smartphone camera use: a day at the Biltmore.

Perhaps the biggest difference between these phones is Vivo’s pursuit of “perfection,” while Google seems to embrace a more “natural” look. At 10x and 5x, the photos from both Vivo phones are essentially noise-free, have clear sharpening applied to them, and are generally quite vibrant. Despite having similar specs, the Vivo X500 Pro’s more advanced Sony LYT telephoto sensor pulls out more detail.

Google’s images are notably softer, although not in a low-resolution sort of way. They look more like they’re taken from a standard camera rather than a smartphone, which some people may prefer. While telephoto image quality between the Vivo X500 Pro and Google Pixel 11 Pro is mostly down to preference, Google’s phone is substantially faster while taking the actual photo. In all of the darker shots, the Pixel 11 Pro took the picture instantly. Both Vivo X500 models took a 2-3 second exposure shot, making me stand there for longer than I’m used to.

Ultrawide performance is where I feel we see the largest gap between these phones. Both the Vivo X500 and X500 Pro cameras offer bright, well-balanced shots that are vibrant but not unrealistic. The light sources in the room emit a lovely warm glow, yet the bulbs themselves aren’t overblown. The X500 Pro’s slightly warmer image across all three scenarios looks best to me.

The Pixel 11 Pro’s ultrawide images are often underexposed and lacking in dynamic range. The staircase shot exhibits this problem the most. This can come off as natural in some scenarios, as modern smartphone photography is notorious for brightening dark areas too much, but too often the Pixel 11 Pro’s ultrawide shots just feel unnaturally dark.

The main camera is much more balanced, although it’s fun to push these phones to find their weaknesses. One of the best examples of differing priorities is in the wreath shot, which was quite dark in real life thanks to the bright sunlight outside. The Pixel 11 Pro valiantly tried to brighten up the shadows so you could actually see the wreath (since it’s clearly the subject), causing the rest of the scene to look forcefully bright.

The same issue cropped up in the image of the wine rack, where both Vivo phones captured a nicer-looking shot. The image of the trellis walkway is a tie, but the photo of my ridiculous bowl of ice cream looks best on the Vivo X500 Pro, once again.

Portrait mode is where I think the Vivo X500 Pro is the clear loser, but it’s not for any of the reasons in the other categories above. Rather, the X500 Pro seems to have some weird warping happening in the bokeh processing that’s not present on the base Vivo X500. This is in both front-facing and rear camera portrait shots, so it’s not a lens issue or some weird setting.

Google cleaned up its portrait shots so much this year that it’s borderline ridiculous. Pixels had the worst portrait mode for years, but this year’s overhaul of the portrait algorithm made all the difference in the world.

Overall, the Vivo X500 series is another solid release for Vivo, and while neither the base nor Pro models offer the same top-tier camera specs as the Pro Max model, it’s clear Vivo pulled off something great. The Vivo X500 Pro retails for €1,399 in the EU, while the base X500 global model is a little harder to find, but should be quite a bit cheaper if you can get your hands on it.

#10612. Google Play wants Android apps to nail foldable basics by April 2027

Google Play is introducing new adaptive app and game quality requirements next year to elevate the Android foldable experience.

Starting April 2027, the Play Store will require developers to meet Tier 3 of the Android adaptive app quality guidelines for foldables.

Referred to as “Adaptive ready” or “basic” support, Tier 3 requires apps not to use letterboxing (or other compatibility modes) and to fill the entire screen. During rotation, resizing, and folding/unfolding, apps must preserve scroll position, text input, and media playback. Apps must also support multi-window environments.

Apps that do not meet this quality bar will no longer be able to publish or update on Google Play, and may have reduced visibility on the store.

At the same time, Google Play will require foldable device screenshots for app listings: “Providing foldable screenshots by April 2027 will become a standard submission requirement when publishing new apps or updating your store listing.”

Curious to see whether the requirements will actually be as strict as they make them seem (on paper).

If so, then also Google will have a ton of work to do to modernise all of their apps.

By August 2027, new and updated apps will be required to target Android 17 (API level 37).

Over time, this “quality framework will progressively expand to incorporate Tier 2 and Tier 1 standards across foldables, tablets, Googlebooks, and Android XR.”

Apps that meet Tier 2 (better) or Tier 1 (best) will get “premier store badging, editorial spotlighting, and enhanced merchandising across Google Play as the platform bar elevates.”

On the gaming front, titles will need to add foldable screenshots to listings by April 2027, while Tier 3 large screen quality guidelines go into effect in August 2027.

#10607. Will Wright’s next game uses AI to map your memories

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

MyMems, from the creator of SimCity and The Sims, is a new social simulator powered by AI.

MyMems, from the creator of SimCity and The Sims, is a new social simulator powered by AI.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Will Wright, the creator of legendary franchises like SimCity and The Sims, is working on a new game — and it’s built around AI. The new title, focused on your memories, is called MyMems. Like Wright’s other titles, it sounds extremely ambitious, giving players a new way to explore worlds they create on their own. “We want to take your autobiography and turn it into a toy world,” he tells The Verge.

The MyMems website describes the mobile game as a “social simulation” with worlds “built from your memories and the people who shaped you.” You can add “memories” to your game, like speaking a short story to your phone, and those memories are generated into pieces you can place on the game’s map. Your worlds can also include AI-powered avatars, which can represent people you know in your life. Ideally, as you play, the planet starts to become a “map of your psyche,” kind of like Inside Out, Wright says. Worlds can be private, but you can also share them with others.

Here’s another way to think of it, from Virginia McArthur, executive producer and GM at MyMems developer Gallium Studios: “It’s a cozy world builder with a really deep social simulation underneath.”

I’ll admit that I don’t totally get it, and it seems like a tricky idea to grasp without playing it. Still, as heady of a concept as it might be — turning your memories into a game world — Wright and team still want it to be fun. “This isn’t meant to be your legacy diary necessarily,” says Anthony Maitz, a product manager at Gallium Studios. “It’s meant to just be something you kind of play around with.” However, Maitz notes that what you’re working toward is a “proxy” of you — a word that seems to be the inspiration of the game’s earlier title, Proxi: Yesterday’s You Tomorrow. The game will use the “Behavior Engine” from a company called Artificial Agency, which makes AI tech for video games, as a “foundational technology.”

Like other industries, gaming is starting to reckon with generative AI. Early AI virtual worlds created with models from a startup backed by a Pixar cofounder and Google’s Project Genie weren’t experiences you’d actually want to spend dedicated time with, and indie developers I spoke to in March were largely opposed to the technology. But AI technology has taken some big leaps this year with companies like Google, Meta, and Roblox now letting users make games with AI right from their phone, and retro gaming becoming overrun with AI-powered ports of classic titles.

Take-Two Interactive CEO Strauss Zelnick said earlier this year that generative AI has “zero part in what Rockstar Games is building” with GTA VI. But even as a recent industry survey indicated that more game developers are starting to use generative AI as part of their job, workers are pushing back against generative AI use, and the technology is at the center of fierce debates over indie game awards.

Many players are rejecting AI outright, and I wondered if the Gallium Studios team was worried about opposition toward MyMems’ use of AI. Wright says he has three boys at home and that they are all “pretty negative” on AI. He attributes negativity over AI to be toward the meaning of art and authorship and credit, a concern he calls “well-founded.” But he also calls AI an “amazing tool.”

“We’re not making slop,” says McArthur. But Maitz notes that while the team has “been concerned” about a negative response to the generative AI use, they’ve learned that people don’t mind when they use AI in a “Scribblenauts-y style” for things like having the game make a high school. The team also found that people got “addicted” to a test app with a character creator, including Maitz’s own daughter, who he says “hates” AI. “By making it so that the player is authoring it over and over, bit by bit, it doesn’t really feel like you cheated,” Maitz says.

MyMems, which is set to launch in 2027, will join a growing field of other social simulations, like Krafton’s Inzoi and Nintendo’s smash hit Tomodachi Life: Living the Dream. But none quite have the pull of The Sims, which remains one of EA’s most important franchises. With The Sims, Wright says that many people tried making their real families in the game.

“It was interesting how people really were naturally trying to map this game, which was like a little toy, onto their real life,” he says. With MyMems, that’s what the team is trying to do the “follow-through” on — what if they made the whole game focused on you?

Imagine opening a family scrapbook and the way people and things jump out, Wright explains. “As you go through more and more pages, pretty soon you have this whole little circus of people and events and things from your life.” It’s chaos, he says. “I want something that feels kind of like that.”

Update, November 18th: Added context about Artificial Agency.

#10605. How to Migrate an Elementor Site to the WordPress Block Editor

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Elementor made your site easy to build. It may also be making it slow. Here's how to move a site onto the Block Editor without breaking URLs, losing content or dropping out of Google.

Page builders solved a real problem. Before the Block Editor matured, they were the easiest way to give clients drag-and-drop layouts. Many of those sites are still running today, and a lot of them are slow on phones.

The data backs that up. According to the HTTP Archive Core Web Vitals Technology Report for June 2026, 36.8% of Elementor sites pass Core Web Vitals on mobile. Sites built with the Block Editor pass 53.0% of the time. That's correlation, not proof, since hosting and setup matter too. But in my experience, it matches what happens when you remove the builder from a slow site.

My reason for migrating sites off Elementor is simple: **page builders make websites heavy. **Having said that, Elementor has improved a lot. Its performance settings, Flexbox Containers, and asset-loading options cut much of the bloat, and a carefully tuned Elementor site can pass Core Web Vitals. But tuning only goes so far. You’re still shipping the builder’s framework, wrapper markup and scripts, and every plugin or Elementor update is a chance for the speed to slip again.

For small business sites that don’t need drag-and-drop editing, rebuilding the same design on the WordPress Block Editor (Gutenberg) removes that weight at the source, without changing what the client sees.

This guide follows the order I use. It assumes you're comfortable with WordPress admin, WP-CLI and a staging site. If you need to set those up first, SitePoint's guide to the ultimate WordPress development environment covers the tooling.

Not every Elementor site needs to move. Migration makes sense when:

It's harder to justify when the site leans on Elementor Pro features you'd need to replace, such as complex popups, dynamic loops or WooCommerce templates. Those can all be rebuilt, but the job gets bigger fast.

You can't prove a migration worked without "before" numbers. Capture these on the live site first.

Crawl the site with a tool such as Screaming Frog, and export every indexable URL with its title, meta description, H1 and canonical tag. You can also pull published pages and posts with WP-CLI:

Run Google PageSpeed Insights, which uses Lighthouse for its lab scores, on the homepage and two or three key templates. Save the mobile lab scores and, if the site has enough traffic, the field data from real users.

Export the last three months of pages and queries from Google Search Console. That's what you'll compare against after launch.

Elementor stores each page's layout as JSON in the elementordata post meta field, and marks builder pages with elementoredit_mode. This command lists every page that uses the builder:

Then list everything else Elementor controls, which is easy to miss:

On the small business sites I've migrated, the inventory usually turns up more than the owner expects. The pages are the easy part. The surprises are saved templates nobody remembers creating, a popup still running on every page, add-on widgets from plugins that were installed once and forgotten, and contact forms that live inside Elementor Pro rather than a form plugin. Finding those before the rebuild saves a broken launch.

Work on a staging copy. Install a block theme, such as Twenty Twenty-Five or a lightweight starter theme, and move the Elementor global styles into theme.json. That way colors, fonts and spacing are defined once, instead of in every widget.

Copy the hex values and font names from Elementor's Site Settings so the new site matches the old one. Rebuild the header and footer as template parts in the Site Editor. If you'd rather write a theme yourself, SitePoint's series on building a WordPress theme from scratch covers the basics.

Most Elementor sites repeat the same few sections: a hero, a services grid, testimonials and a call to action. Build each one once as a block pattern, then reuse it on every page. Patterns are plain block markup, so you don't need React to write them, whatever you may have heard about React and WordPress development.

In a block theme, any PHP file in the /patterns folder with a header comment is registered automatically:

For page content, there's a useful shortcut. Elementor saves a plain HTML copy of each page into post_content. Open a page in the Block Editor, and the old content usually appears in a Classic block. Choose Convert to blocks, then delete the leftovers and rebuild the layout with your patterns. It keeps the copy and images, so you only redo the design.

The difference shows up in the page source. Here's typical front-end markup for a single heading on an Elementor page, using the older section and column layout:

Newer Elementor versions with Flexbox containers and the optimized markup settings trim some of those wrappers, but most older sites still carry all of them. Multiply that by every element on every page, and the weight adds up.

To speed up this step, I built a free Elementor to Gutenberg converter. Paste in a page's elementordata JSON from Step 2, and it returns block markup you can paste into the Block Editor's code editor. It handles headings, text, images, buttons, spacers, dividers and column layouts, and lists anything it can't convert, such as forms and popups, for you to rebuild by hand. It runs entirely in your browser, so the page data never leaves your computer.

The code is rarely what holds a migration up. Content is. Once clients see their site being rebuilt, many decide it's time to rewrite their pages too, and the project ends up waiting on copywriters and approvals. My fix is to separate the two: migrate the existing content as it is, launch, then update the copy afterwards. That keeps the speed and SEO gains on schedule, and it means any ranking change can be traced to either the migration or the new copy, not both at once.

This is the step that decides whether rankings hold.

To spot-check a redirect or a status code from the command line:

When staging matches the live site, deploy it. Then:

Leave the old elementordata meta in place for a few weeks, in case you need to roll back. Once you're confident, take a backup and remove it to shrink the database.

Run PageSpeed Insights again straight away for lab scores. Field data takes longer: the Chrome UX Report uses a rolling 28-day window, so give it a month before judging real-user results. Compare Search Console clicks for the same period before and after launch.

In my migrations, the biggest change is usually page weight and loading time, because the builder's CSS, JavaScript and wrapper markup are gone. What doesn't always improve is server response time. If the site is on slow shared hosting, the first byte still arrives late, and that needs a hosting fix, not a rebuild. Search traffic should hold steady if the URLs and metadata were protected. If it drops, the Step 5 crawl comparison is the first place to look.

Moving off Elementor isn't a one-click job, but it isn't a full redesign either. Record a baseline, inventory what the builder controls, rebuild with patterns, and protect every URL. Most of the speed gain comes from removing code the site never needed. For what to tackle after that, see these practical web performance techniques. Most of the risk comes from rushing the SEO checks. Done carefully, the client gets the same site, a faster one, and an editor that comes with WordPress itself.

***Author Bio: ***Fahad Raza is a WordPress developer and SEO consultant based in Sydney. He has built websites since 2005, worked in SEO for more than 18 years, and spent eight and a half years on the IKEA Australia and New Zealand website.

#10604. Review: Amazon’s Alexa Tablet 8 is a Fire Tablet with Google Play for $150 – what’s not to love?

Amazon shocked everyone in October when it launched its new Alexa Tablet series with Google Play support built-in, addressing the biggest shortcoming of its older Fire Tablets. For Black Friday 2026, the Alexa Tablet 8 is a mere $150 and, with Google Play on board, this feels like a Fire Tablet that’s all grown up.

The Alexa Tablet 8 is one of three new Amazon tablets that launched in October with Google Play on board, and it’s also the one I was personally the most excited about. I love a good tiny Android tablet, but there just aren’t that many out there – and some of the ones we do have are exorbitantly expensive.

Right up there with Amazon’s long-running reputation in the tablet space, Alexa Tablet 8 is very much an affordable device, and that’s reflected in its fairly meager specs:

Yet, I didn’t find myself put off by the device.

I specifically asked Amazon for a review unit of the absolute base model – 4GB of RAM and lockscreen ads – and I can say with confidence it’s Not Bad at the end of the day.

The display is objectively not good, but it’s not really trying to be flashy either. The panel gets bright enough for most indoor use and, for general browsing or watching videos around the house or even on a flight, it gets the job done. If anything, my biggest complaint on the display was just how smudgey it gets with fingerprints.

Hardware is a strong suit. The Alexa Tablet 8 has an aluminum build that comes in this gorgeous teal color as well as a softer “Rosette” pink (or “Graphite,” if you want to keep it boring). It’s thin and light and slips into a bag easily, and even fit into the back pocket of my jeans. Buttons are clicky and aspects such as the speakers actually do well enough – like the display, the speakers aren’t good, but they’re not bad by any stretch either.

Amazon has a folio cover available that’s simple but nice, with a fabric finish that’s grippy. It’s an extra $40, though, which feels like a bit much for a super-affordable Android slate.

The biggest question was around the software experience, and this is where this new effort really shines.

Alexa Tablet 8 ships with Android 16 out of the box, not FireOS or whatever Amazon was calling its forked experience on Fire Tablets (I genuinely refuse to go double-check because it’s a memory I’m thrilled to never have to revisit). It’s a pretty “stock” experience, with Amazon leaving aspects such as the notification shade/quick settings alone, and even the Settings menu feels pretty clean.

That’s not to say nothing has changed. The homescreen has a fairly standard layout , but with these neat little corner widgets – one a “dynamic” widget with scrolling content (mostly ads) and the other a shortcut for Alexa search. Amazon is pushing Alexa as a voice assistant pretty heavily on these tablets, but I’m happy to report it’s pretty easy to ignore if you choose. These tablets will be great additions to your Alexa ecosystem if you’re invested, but I’m simply not.

The biggest change you’ll find is the lockscreen, where Amazon’s base models all come with lockscreen ads. This saves you about $20 on the hardware, and it’s not that bad of an experience. That said, I’d absolutely spend the extra $20 at purchase to go without them, especially seeing as this blocks the ability to set a lockscreen wallpaper at all. It doesn’t cost anything more to remove them later, though, with a $20 charge through your Amazon account removing ads.

But what’s important here is that, for the fire time, Amazon is admitting defeat and installing the Google Play Store.

Alexa Tablet 8, and the rest of the lineup, has Google Play out of the box and access to any app you want. This means that you can, if you choose, fully ignore Amazon’s own services and do whatever you want. The smallest of examples: While Amazon’s built-in keyboard is fine, it’s wonderful that I don’t have to jump through hoops to switch over to Gboard. Fire Tablets were always an enticing value with a big catch, but Amazon’s change in this regard truly makes Alexa Tablets an attractive option for everyone – not just those willing to put up with the restriction or put in the work to hack Google apps on board.

Performance, despite the meager specs, is not awful. MediaTek’s low-end chip has just enough horsepower for general use, and 4GB of RAM isn’t as detrimental as I would have expected. It’s not fast by any means, but you could do a lot worse. There are 4GB Android tablets I’ve literally decided not to review because I couldn’t use them past the setup process, so the fact that Amazon is delivering a completely usable experience is a win in my book, even more so that I don’t actually mind using it. There’s a caveat to that, of course, and it’s that you shouldn’t expect to do a ton on this base model. For light browsing, reading Kindle books (or whatever other reading app since, you know, Google Play), or watching videos, this is great. For games, you’ll want to look elsewhere (and expect to spend more).

Alexa Tablet 8 isn’t some shining example of a perfect budget tablet, but I think it really nails the fundamentals. At its usual $230~ starting price, it’s a plenty-good deal for those in the market for an affordable Android slate, but the Black Friday pricing of just $150 is all the better – I just wish the model with 8GB of RAM was also on sale.

Amazon’s pivot to Alexa Tablets is great – though I hope the company knows full well that everyone is still going to call these Fire Tablets or Kindles or whatever combination of brands that spring to mind. But that reputation might work in Amazon’s favor at the end of the day. While there’s a huge portion of the audience that’s been left frustrated for years at the lack of Play Store support on Fire Tablets, they’ve still been a market success for the most part, so this shift is just a win for everyone. Users get a better experience, Amazon gets to shuffle its priorities away from a dead app store, and the market as a whole gets an affordable piece of hardware that doesn’t suck.

#10601. Google Pixel officially re-releases App Lock in beta, Samsung Galaxy gets it too

It’s been a wild ride for Google Pixel’s App Lock feature, but the company is officially re-releasing the feature today, right alongside its debut on Samsung Galaxy devices.

The feature is still only in Android 17 QPR3 Beta 3 for Google Pixel devices, an update that should be released to the stable track in early 2027.

So some Samsung phones will get it before Pixel phones 🤣

You can still access App Lock by long-pressing on an app icon and adding it to the app lock list.

In a post on Reddit, Google’s Community Engagement Manager details that App Lock on Google Pixel was removed for improvements before getting a full release:

…the product team decided to temporarily pull back the feature from the QPR2 Beta to allow for additional polish, thorough testing, and robust integration across all system surfaces.

That’s why Samsung’s One UI 9.5 beta release today also brings support for App Lock. The feature is built into Android 17 QPR2, seemingly the release that One UI 9.5 is based on, but Google Pixel devices aren’t getting it until QPR3. As far as release timelines go, this means that Samsung’s Galaxy S27 series will probably ship with this feature out of the box, where Pixel phones won’t get it until around March or so. One UI 9.5 won’t roll out for older Galaxy devices until May, if One UI 8.5 serves as an example.

#10600. 8BitDo’s new gamepad modernizes the Nintendo GameCube’s WaveBird

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Diehard GameCube fans can connect the controller to their original console, or use it with the Switch.

Diehard GameCube fans can connect the controller to their original console, or use it with the Switch.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The 8BitDo 64 was a loose remake of the Nintendo 64’s unusual triple-grip controller that did away with that gamepad’s centered analog stick. But with its new GC Bluetooth Gamepad, 8BitDo has done a much better job at staying faithful to the design of the Nintendo GameCube’s excellent controllers, including their unique button layout, thumbstick design, and shoulder buttons.

The 8BitDo GC Bluetooth Gamepad is available for preorder starting today for $49.99 in orange or purple and is expected to start shipping on December 10th. That’s much cheaper than Nintendo’s $69.99 wireless remake of the GameCube controller, although 8BitDo’s version isn’t an exact copy of the original’s shape and ergonomics. If that’s important to you, and you have your GameCube’s original accessories on hand, you can still buy 8BitDo’s $25.98 upgrade kit, which is a cheaper way to make it compatible with modern consoles and devices.

8BitDo’s GameCube redux carries forward the original’s octagonal gates around the base of each thumbstick, making it well-suited to playing fighting games like Super Smash Bros. Ultimate. And while Nintendo continues to skip modern thumbstick technologies, the 8BitDo GC features both drift-resistant TMR thumbsticks and Hall effect linear triggers.

The controller connects to all versions of the Nintendo Switch over Bluetooth, as well as PCs or handheld consoles running Windows. It also comes with a 2.4GHz wireless USB receiver for players who prefer a connection with lower latency, or you can connect it with a USB-C cable for the best possible performance.

#10597. Three 'e-waste' Nvidia V100s hit 100 tokens per second on Qwen 3.6 35B in a $1,042 homelab rig

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

An M5 Max Mac Studio is slightly faster at short context, but the V100s pull ahead at 64K and longer.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A homelab builder benchmarked three used Nvidia V100s on Qwen 3.6 35B, achieving about 100 tokens per second in a custom setup costing $1,042 with just “e-waste” GPUs. The smaller, dense Qwen 3.6 27B manages about 24 tokens per second on the same platform, and the V100s use about half the energy of the same number of P100s for a given job.

The blog post, “Scaling ‘E-Waste’: LLMs on Multiple Decommissioned GPUs,” compares nine different builds — one to three K80s, P100s, or V100-16GB cards — on a single Supermicro dual-Xeon board. The test uses llama.cpp and covers more than 130 LLM tests, including Qwen 2.5, Gemma 4, and, of course, Qwen 3.6, at Q4/Q8/F16 precisions and from 4K to 128K context sizes. GPU energy per run is also recorded. The builder is “daily driving Qwen 3.6 35B” on the V100s and notes that scaling decommissioned GPUs will only get you so far.

“E-waste,” in this case, refers to decommissioned enterprise GPUs, which are a popular but somewhat underpowered option when compared to very expensive modern alternatives. The Nvidia Tesla V100s in question are tested as a follow-up to a July round which tested 15 “e-waste” GPUs, and this time the board and CPUs stay the same for every build — the Supermicro X10DRG-Q with two Intel Xeon E5-2687W 12-core CPUs, powered by the Corsair HX1500i PSU.

Performance is judged based on what is considered “realtime interaction” which, here, is 70 tokens per second. As to why the number of GPUs is three: Qwen 3.6 35B, the primary tested model, fails on just one or two at Q8, or 8-bit quantization.

Here are the given prices, with the note that they aren’t “100% reflective of what you could find on eBay today,” although the totals count only one of the two CPUs and omit RAM, storage, the case, and any cooling.

The older cards are relatively inexpensive because they “run hot and ship without cooling,” which required the builder to 3D print one blower duct per card and has led to the development of a standalone cooler.

The bench itself involves 10 runs of 512 generated tokens per test with context pre-filled to 4K, 32K, 64K, or 128K. Qwen 3.6 35B is a mixture-of-experts (MoE) model, which only activates about 3 billion out of its 35 billion total parameters per token. The numbers that immediately follow are at Q8.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

The token goal was easily reached…when the context window was small, or 4K. The 100.8 tokens per second there fell to 72.7 at 128K, still usable by the 70 tokens per second goal. The 27B model, which is not MoE, fared much worse, at 24.3 at 4K, 21.6 at 32K, 18.7 at 64K, and just 14.2 at 128K. The MoE ends up about four to five times faster, depending on the context size.

Also tested were other dense models of about 30B parameters. At 4K, Gemma 4 31B achieved 20.9 and Qwen 2.5 32B 21.0, while the MoE Gemma 4 26B cleared the 70 tokens per second bar with just two V100s at Q8. If you move down to Q4, or 4-bit quantization, two V100s can run Qwen 3.6 35B at 77.1 to 105.4 tokens per second, between 128K and 4K context size. This is where it’s claimed that adding more cards gives you more memory, which enables larger models to be run, but not speed.

The P100s, for comparison, only hit 40.2–55.2 tokens per second in the same context range with Q8 Qwen 3.6 35B, and the target 70 only with Qwen 2.5 3B. If we compare to more modern hardware — the Nvidia DGX Spark, MSRP $4,700, and the M5 Max Mac Studio, which starts at $2,499 but costs $5,399 with the tested 40-core GPU, 128GB of memory, and a 1TB SSD — the 3x V100 build holds its own, using sourced data.

The Mac comparison comes from the oMLX benchmark page for the M5 Max with 40 GPU cores and 128GB of unified memory, and that hits 43.9 tokens per second with a ~195K-token prompt on the Qwen 3.6 35B model. The Mac is slightly ahead at 4K and 32K, while the V100s pull ahead at 64K and 128K, by 19% at the longest context. These numbers have to be taken loosely, as the machines use different runtimes.

The Spark run came from Kleybrink’s dgx-spark-bench, using Ollama in thinking mode with short prompts. Its 45.8 tokens per second is less than half the V100s’ 4K figure.

Going further to modern GPUs, which “destroy our decommissioned equivalents,” as the builder puts it, you get about 315 tokens per second on two H100s and about 455 on one B200 at 128K, the B200 roughly six times the V100 box, even though the hosted cards ran the full 16-bit model against the V100s’ 8-bit. Parasail, an inference provider, sponsored that comparison with dedicated H100 and B200 deployments.

As for energy, the V100 setup came out to about 0.02 kWh for a 128K 35B run, with the P100s taking twice that much and two or three K80s as much as 0.35 to 0.45 kWh. The V100-16GB simply “[puts] up better scores while consuming less energy,” in comparison to the P100s. These numbers are for the GPUs and not from the wall.

There’s more to come. The builder wants a benchmark that “maps to ‘software factory’ performance,” a term for models being prompted constantly, around the clock, where instant speed matters less. Then there’s the cooler, which is “almost ready for release,” with beta testers to be recruited from the mailing list, although no date has been given. From the Reddit thread, the builder mentions the intention to compare inference backends and use speculative decoding to improve performance.

#10593. Google is forcing Android app developers to properly support foldables

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Now that the iPhone Duo is foldable is here, Google wants to make sure apps on Android foldables keep up.

Now that the iPhone Duo is foldable is here, Google wants to make sure apps on Android foldables keep up.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Now that Apple’s foldable is here, Google wants to make sure apps on Android foldables are a good experience. Starting in April 2027, Google is going to require Android developers to make sure their apps have a baseline level of support for foldables. If they don’t, developers risk not being able to publish or update their apps or have reduced visibility for their apps on Google Play.

Google says Android apps must meet its “Tier 3 — Adaptive ready” standard. “Apps must fill the available display area without letterboxing or compatibility modes,” Google says. “Apps must preserve state (scroll position, text input, media playback) during rotation, folding/unfolding, and resizing, while supporting multi-window environments.” (The other two tiers are “Adaptive optimized” and “Adaptive differentiated” and take further advantage of the possibilities of foldables.) As of the April 2027 deadline, developers also will have to include three foldable screenshots for their app listings. Developers can start publishing their foldable screenshots today.

Similar requirements will apply to games, though developers have until August 2027 to get their titles ready for Android foldables, Google says. (Google “plans to extend” its requirements to other large-screen devices like tablets and Googlebook laptops in “future phases.”) However, game developers will still need to share three foldable screenshots for app listings starting in April.

The changes are being announced just weeks after Apple launched the iPhone Duo, which already has more than 200,000 optimized apps, though some developers are still figuring out Apple’s foldable form factor.

#10590. Automating Contract Signing in Node.js: Webhooks, Verification, and Storing Signed PDFs

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Sending a document for signature is the easy part of an e-signature integration. One POST request and the signer gets an email. The work that actually breaks in production happens afterwards: knowing when the document was signed, trusting the notification that told you so, handling the same notification twice, and getting the final PDF into storage before the download link expires.

This tutorial builds that whole loop in Node.js with Express. An event in your app (a deal is approved, a client is onboarded, a task moves to "ready to sign") sends a contract from a template. When the signer finishes, a webhook arrives, your server verifies it, and the signed PDF plus its audit trail get saved without anyone touching them.

The code uses Firma.dev, the e-signature API I work on, because I needed a real API to make the examples runnable. The patterns are not specific to it, though. Signature verification, idempotent handlers, short-lived download URLs, and reconciliation jobs show up in almost every webhook-driven integration, and the same structure applies whether you're receiving events from a payment processor, a CI system, or another signing provider.

There's a fifth part that tutorials usually skip: a reconciliation job that catches anything the webhooks missed. We'll build that too, because webhooks are a notification mechanism, not a delivery guarantee.

Before sending anything, wrap the HTTP calls in one place. This is where authentication lives, and it's also where you handle 429 Too Many Requests once instead of in every caller. Most APIs, this one included, return a Retry-After header on a 429, so the client should honour it rather than guessing a backoff.

The 503 branch matters later. When a document has just been completed, the final PDF and its completion certificate take a moment to generate, and the download endpoint says so with a 503 and a Retry-After instead of handing you a half-built file.

The trigger depends on your product. It might be a route handler, a queue consumer, or a CRM automation calling into your service. What matters is that the function doing the sending is small and takes plain data:

Two design choices are worth calling out.

Use a single create-and-send call when you can. Many signing APIs split this into "create a draft" and "send the draft." That's useful when a human needs to review the document first, but for automation it creates a window where your process can crash between the two calls and leave an orphaned draft. A combined endpoint makes the operation atomic from your side.

Store the provider's ID on your own record immediately. Webhook payloads reference the signing request, not your deal or customer. Without that mapping you'll end up searching by email address, which falls apart as soon as one client signs two documents.

Here's the first place integrations quietly go wrong. Webhook signatures are computed over the exact bytes the sender transmitted. If Express parses the body as JSON first, you lose those bytes, and re-serialising the object won't reproduce them (key order, whitespace, and number formatting can all change). Signature checks then fail intermittently, which is the worst kind of failure.

The fix is to use express.raw() on the webhook route only, so the handler receives a Buffer:

Most webhook providers sign payloads with HMAC-SHA256 using a shared secret. The details vary, so read your provider's docs for the exact header format, but the scheme here is common: the header looks like t=1731456000,v1=5f2b..., and the signed string is the timestamp, a dot, and the raw body. Stripe uses a very similar format, and the Standard Webhooks spec describes the same idea as a vendor-neutral convention.

Each line here defends against something specific.

The OWASP guidance on cryptographic storage covers the other half of this: the secret belongs in an environment variable or secrets manager, never in the repository.

Every serious webhook system delivers at least once, not exactly once. If your endpoint is slow, returns a 5xx, or the network drops the response, the sender retries. Firma.dev, for example, makes up to three attempts per event (immediately, then after at least five minutes, then after at least an hour) and treats anything other than a 2xx as a failure, including redirects. Other providers retry more aggressively. Either way, your handler will eventually see the same event twice.

The defence is to record each event's unique ID and skip ones you've seen. In the server above that's alreadyProcessed and markProcessed. For a demo, a Set works:

In production, use your database and let a unique constraint do the work, so two concurrent deliveries can't both pass the check:

Also notice the order in the route: respond with 200, then do the work. Many providers time out deliveries after a few seconds (five, in this case). Downloading a PDF and uploading it to object storage can easily take longer than that, and a timeout counts as a failure, which triggers a retry, which triggers duplicate work. Acknowledging fast and processing asynchronously avoids that loop.

setImmediate is fine for a tutorial, but it has a real weakness: if the process dies after sending the 200 and before finishing the work, the event is acknowledged and lost. In production, push the event onto a durable queue (BullMQ, SQS, a jobs table) before responding. Step 7 covers the safety net for whatever still slips through.

Now the actual business logic. You'll usually see several event types (sent, viewed, a signer signed, declined, expired, completed). Only one of them means "the document is final":

Three things in here are easy to get wrong.

Don't treat "a signer signed" as "the document is done." With multiple signers, each one triggers its own event, and a per-signer "all complete" flag can be true on more than one of them. Key your storage logic off the single completion event.

Don't persist the download URL. Pre-signed URLs expire (this one carries an expires_at). Saving the link to your database and serving it to users later produces broken links a few minutes or hours later. Download the bytes and store them in storage you control. Swapping the fs.writeFile calls for an S3 PutObject or a Google Drive upload is a drop-in change.

Store the audit trail, not just the PDF. If a signature is ever disputed, the PDF shows what was signed, and the audit trail shows who signed it, when, and from where (timestamps, IP addresses, the sequence of views and signatures). That evidence is what makes an electronic signature defensible, which brings us to the legal side.

Retries are finite. Endpoints go down during deploys. Some providers auto-disable a webhook after enough consecutive failures, and events aren't guaranteed to arrive in order. Any one of these can leave a completed document sitting unsaved.

A small scheduled job closes that gap. Keep a list of signing requests your app considers "pending," and periodically ask the API for their real status:

Since storeSignedDocument overwrites by ID, running it twice for the same document is harmless. That's the property you want from every step in a webhook pipeline: doing it again shouldn't change the result.

None of this is legal advice, but a few facts shape how you build.

In the US, the ESIGN Act and the Uniform Electronic Transactions Act (adopted by 49 states) give electronic signatures the same legal standing as handwritten ones for most commercial documents. ESIGN also has requirements around consumer consent and record retention, which is part of why you store the final PDF yourself rather than relying on a third-party link.

In the EU, eIDAS defines three levels: simple (SES), advanced (AdES), and qualified (QES). SES and AdES cover most business contracts. QES requires a qualified trust service provider and certificate-based identity, and some documents (certain property transfers and court filings, depending on the country) require it or exclude e-signatures entirely. Check which level your document types need before you pick a provider, since not every API offers every level.

Across jurisdictions, the evidence that holds up is the same: who signed, how they were identified, when it happened, and proof the document hasn't changed since. That's the audit trail and the completion certificate, which is why Step 6 stores both.

If you're evaluating providers for a workflow like this, the questions that matter at the integration level are mostly not on the pricing page:

The send call is one line. The reliability comes from everything around it: keeping the raw body for verification, checking timestamps and comparing in constant time, acknowledging fast and deduplicating by event ID, downloading files before their links expire, and running a reconciliation job for the events that never arrive. Get those right and contract signing becomes something your app does in the background, with no one chasing signatures or copying PDFs between folders.

For the Firma.dev-specific details used here (event names, header format, rate limits), see the webhooks guide.

With Grand Theft Auto VI set to finally launch tomorrow, November 19th, our long-awaited return to Vice City is nearly here. But after all this time, some of the gear you’ve used in San Andreas might feel out of place on the glamorous streets of Leonidas County. Luckily for you, we’ve gathered a few of our favorite Xbox and PlayStation 5 accessories to help your long gaming sessions run as smoothly as possible.

The 8BitDo Ultimate 3E — an upgraded pick for Xbox Series X and Series S consoles — is a very capable, comfortable controller with elite-level features, including a high polling rate and adjustable triggers. It’s on the expensive side at $149.99, but the customization and clean execution justify the cost. Read our review.

PlayStation 5 owners looking for a controller upgrade, meanwhile, might consider the DualSense Edge, Sony’s first-party pro-style controller. In addition to a variety of extra function buttons and paddles, the wireless gamepad sports adjustable trigger heights and swappable joysticks (in case the potentiometers start to drift). It’s a little bit heavier than Sony’s regular DualSense, and the battery life isn’t quite as good, but it offers customization you won’t find on the console’s default controller. Read our review.

Sony’s pro-style controller, the DualSense Edge, might suffer from shorter battery life than the standard DualSense model, but it features great software integration with the PlayStation 5 and plenty of customizable components.

Nothing’s worse than having to pause a heist so you can go hunt down a fresh set of AA batteries. The PowerA Duo Charging Station includes a rechargeable battery and replacement rear doors for two Xbox Series X / S or Xbox One controllers. Simply set them on the dock, and they’ll start charging.

Each Pro Series panel also sports a 120Hz refresh rate, four HDMI 2.1 ports, and both low latency and variable refresh rates for gaming.

For a more premium offering, the Sony Bravia 8 II is one of our favorite screens for movies, with an engaging, immersive picture and excellent image processing. It has great gaming features, too, like a 120Hz refresh rate with variable refresh and an automatic low-latency mode. It’s not cheap, but the OLED makes for an excellent upgrade to your setup. Read our review.

Sony’s latest first-party headset, the $99 PlayStation Pulse Wireless, launches the same day as GTA VI. My colleague Cameron Faulkner recently went hands-on with the wireless headset ahead of its launch on November 19th, and came away impressed with its overall audio quality and comfort. The newest Pulse is also straightforward to set up and use, especially when paired with devices like the PlayStation Portal that support PlayStation Link, Sony’s proprietary low-latency audio connection. It also offers Bluetooth for use with other devices.

For Xbox gamers, it’s hard to beat the SteelSeries Arctis Nova 3X, which retails in multiple colors for $109.99 or less. The wireless headset is lightweight, comfortable, and compatible with a wide variety of other consoles and mobile devices as well, for those rare moments when you aren’t playing GTA VI.

If you’d rather the simplicity of a wired option, Sony’s InZone H6 Air skips the batteries and dongles, instead opting to plug straight into your controller. This open-back gaming headset is lightweight and comfortable, which is great for long hours and late nights. My colleague Cameron Faulkner described its sound as “spectacular,” with several equalizer options available for helping fine-tune the sound to your liking. Read our review.

Ikea’s Poäng chair balances affordability and comfort, with wide, supportive armrests that are just the right height for holding a controller. It comes in a variety of frame materials and cushion colors, too, making it easy to match your other furniture or console. This is my personal chair for console gaming, and I recently added a matching ottoman to the set for maximum relaxation.

#10587. Calling in sick: Vice City

Inbox is written by Daniel Bader. Read previous issues here, and then catch up with Ben Schoon’s excellent Weekender column published every Sunday.

Not sure if you’ve heard, but the biggest video game of all time is out today: buying Anthropic stock. Yes, in case you haven’t already blown all of your money on the GTA VI Ultimate Edition, you can try your luck at buying one of the most anticipated IPOs of all time when ANT goes public on the Nasdaq later today.

But you’re likely up, playing the game that is, according to Metacritic, already among the best of all time. It’s currently sitting at a near-perfect 99/100 on the aggregator, with reviewers calling it “a masterpiece” and “gaming’s… greatest love story.” It’s a game in which you can do basically anything you want, from the violent to the vacuous.

More than anything, though, I’m just excited to discover all the different ways the game allows you to find and consume coffee.

As I’m sure happened in every newsroom, the conversation this week turned to whether traffic will meaningfully decline today as up to 1.5 million people “call in sick” to play GTA VI. If you’ve been awake since the crack of dawn trying to meander your way to Vice City, you’re just one of many folks experiencing the rarest of moments in 2026: a singular, unifying cultural event.

My biggest question isn’t whether gamers will play GTA VI but whether normies will. Previous games in the series, particularly GTA V, sold hundreds of millions of copies and still draw countless players today. GTA VI feels even more inviting to those who just want to spend time in its sandbox to do some fishing, dancing, or flying. One reviewer even called it a “cozy game” where he just spent time walking around a zoo.

While the size, scale, and impact of GTA VI are exponentially higher, the last time I heard people talking about a game in this way was during the pandemic, when everyone was hunkering down for a winter of Animal Crossing.

By the way, GTA VI has a functioning smartphone that can be used for making calls, sending texts, taking photos, looking at “Instagram,” and setting reminders, among other things. It’s yet another minor detail in a game full of them. Plus, it looks like an original Pixel.

Usually with sponsored content, I have to spend the whole time convincing you that the product is worth buying. But RedPocket’s Black Friday deals do all that work on their own.

Not only do you get amazing discounts for the first 12 months of service by prepaying for a full year, but RedPocket’s “Lock-in Low” promise means that you get to stay as close to that promotional price as possible once the first year is over.

There’s no contract and no port-in requirement. You can keep your number, keep your phone, and activate with an eSIM on compatible devices. During the Black Friday sale, there’s even a Refer-a-Friend credit that gets you $25 for every person you bring to the service.

Explore RedPocket’s plans or compare the annual offers. This is one of the best Black Friday decisions you’re going to make this month, and maybe ever.

Google has finally rolled out its answer to Grok Bot, ChatGPT Dots, and Claude Duet, and it’s called… Tasks? Spark has transitioned to Tasks, and the features are rolling out in six countries for all free Gemini users. I’ll have more to say about this next week.

Also interesting is that Google has released yet another AI model, called Neon, that essentially fits between its brand new Carbon model (which isn’t yet widely available, and which ostensibly replaces Argon) and Hydrogen. Confused yet? Don’t be. It’s easy.

If older (and freer) games are more to your taste, someone ported Unreal Tournament 2004 to the browser, where I just spent a few minutes reliving some of my favorite gaming moments of my youth.

These charts showing Nvidia’s growth over the past few years are astounding, enraging, and a little scary. Those are big numbers.

#10586. Ukraine deploys homemade 'flying modems' in daring drone attack to destroy Crimean Bridge, ditching Elon Musk's Starlink

Join for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation plus sign you up to our newsletter.
